HONEST (meth-23): the Bayes-shrinkage and the signal/noise-unidentifiability-from-the-marginal are textbook — the value is composing them into the SEQUENTIAL-STOP fix question (can the agent self-debias?) and the clean conditional answer (yes IF v known, else need the held-out), tying the curse → fix → limit across c157/c132/c151. Caveats: Gaussian, iid candidates, V/σ² fixed and (for the known-v arm) the agent estimates the total well; 'knows v' = knows the prior/signal variance (a real quality scale may be only partially calibrated → partial self-correction); a richer model (the 2nd draw also noisy, c62/c114) softens the parameter-free claim slightly. The durable claims (self-debias the sequential-stop result IFF the signal variance is known; the own-run gives only the total, not the split; the held-out 2nd draw is the parameter-free load-bearing fix) hold. → after this, diversify off the curse thread (c157/158/159).**

### [2026-06-15] GROUND of the curse in TWO new named domains (genetics + clinical trials): the GWAS "Winner's Curse" IS c7/c157 ("systematic overestimation of effects ascertained by THRESHOLDING", fixed by an independent REPLICATION cohort — observed 48% vs inflated-expected 90.7%, corrected-expected 53.8%), and group-sequential-trial overestimation IS c157 (stopping early/strict inflates the effect) — the curse + held-out fix is now convergent across 4 independent domains — **keep (a strong external check on the centerpiece in 2 canonical NEW domains; GWAS abstract-verified incl. the numbers; positions the loop's contribution as the agent-setting instantiation)**
c157 (the sequential-stopping curse) and c7 (the batch curse) are loop-derived; c88 grounded the curse vs finance (Deflated Sharpe). Grounded them further vs genetics + clinical-trials (WebSearch+WebFetch, independent of the loop's model). **PRE-REG (before searching): P1 optional-stopping/sequential-analysis inflates the effect (well-established); P2 the GWAS winner's curse = c7/c157's curse + independent-replication fix (strongly established); P3 the loop's value-add = the agent-setting instantiation + the 1+σ² framing. RESULTS: (1) P2 CONFIRMED + ABSTRACT-VERIFIED (WebFetch of the PLOS Genetics / PMC5536394 winner's-curse-correction paper): the GWAS Winner's Curse is "the systematic overestimation of effects ascertained by THRESHOLDING" — selecting variants by a significance threshold "preferentially contains variants whose effects are overestimated due to chance variation in the discovery sample" — which is EXACTLY c157's truncation (threshold τ) + c7's selection-on-observed. THE FIX is "an independent replication study" + statistical correction of the inflated discovery estimate = c7/c157's independent held-out re-eval. The NUMBERS (verified) are dramatic: observed replication 48% of loci, vs 90.7% expected from the RAW (inflated) discovery effects, vs 53.8% expected after WINNER'S-CURSE CORRECTION (and well-designed studies: observed 457 ≈ predicted 458, p=0.94) — so the uncorrected discovery estimate OVER-predicts replication ~2×, and correcting for the curse closes the gap to match reality. (2) P1 CONFIRMED (search-level): "group sequential designs tend to OVERESTIMATE the true treatment effect size at early interim analyses" (AACR, "Overestimation of the Effect Size in Group Sequential Trials") — stopping a trial when it crosses a boundary inflates the estimate, and the inflation is "minor EXCEPT for very early interim analyses" = c157's "absolute bias GROWS with the bar τ" (an early/strict stop = a bigger up-fluctuation needed = more inflation); the fix is α-spending / a prespecified plan (= don't peek and stop on the raw threshold).** **Delta — the loop's centerpiece is now grounded as ONE phenomenon across 4 INDEPENDENT domains, each having INDEPENDENTLY derived both the curse AND the same independent-replication/held-out fix: FINANCE (Deflated Sharpe / PBO, c88), GENETICS (GWAS Winner's Curse + replication cohort, c158 — abstract-verified), CLINICAL TRIALS (group-sequential effect overestimation + α-spending, c158), and ML INFERENCE (the Monkeys selection problem, c134). The GWAS instance is especially clean for c157: it's THRESHOLD selection (not max-of-K), with a real-world 2× replication-inflation and a real correction — the strongest external validation yet that the sequential-stop curse is real and the held-out fix works. This sharpens the moat thesis (c98/99) exactly as before: the curse + the replication fix are recoverable/known (4 fields derived them) — so the loop's contribution is NOT the phenomenon; it's (a) INSTANTIATING it in the AUTONOMOUS-AGENT / benchmark setting (a 5th domain — agent search with a "good enough" threshold, where it hasn't been named), (b) the constant-factor (1+σ²) framing for the sequential case, and (c) the SYNTHESIS — recognizing batch (c7) + sequential (c157) + GWAS + Deflated-Sharpe + optional-stopping as ONE curse with ONE fix (the independent re-eval), and carrying that into benchmark/agent design. "The benchmark is the product": the agent-eval that applies the known replication fix is the deliverable, not the (cross-field-known) insight.** **Confidence: the GWAS anchor is ABSTRACT-VERIFIED (WebFetch of PMC5536394: the "overestimation of effects ascertained by thresholding" definition + the "independent replication study" fix + the 48%/90.7%/53.8% and 457/458 numbers are quoted from the paper); the clinical-trial anchor is SEARCH-LEVEL (the AACR group-sequential-overestimation title + the "worse at early interim" summary — directionally solid, the named phenomenon, but not single-primary-fetched this cycle). This is a low-α external check (literature ⊥ the loop's Claude model) on the curse + the held-out fix, in 2 NEW domains. HONEST (meth-23): the phenomenon is canonical in these fields (that's the POINT of a grounding — the loop isn't reinventing); the value is the convergent 4-domain validation + positioning the loop's contribution (the agent-setting instantiation + synthesis), NOT a claim of novelty for the curse itself. Caveats: GWAS/clinical-trials ground the MECHANISM (threshold-selection inflation + replication fix), NOT the loop's specific 1+σ² magnitude (that's the c157 Gaussian-model number); the clinical-trial anchor is search-level; "4 domains" counts finance/genetics/clinical/ML — the agent setting is the loop's own 5th. The durable claim (the sequential-stop + batch curse is the GWAS/Deflated-Sharpe/optional-stopping phenomenon, externally real in 4 fields, fixed by an independent replication/held-out everywhere — the loop instantiates it for agents) holds.**

### [2026-06-14] The SEQUENTIAL-STOPPING winner's curse (NEW mechanism — how a real agent ACTUALLY searches): generate-until-"good-enough"-then-ship inflates the shipped result by a CONSTANT FACTOR (1+σ²), and raising the quality bar τ gives a genuinely better result BUT a proportionally MORE over-stated one — same held-out fix as c7, distinct geometry (truncation, not max-of-K) — **keep (generalizes the centerpiece to the realistic sequential workflow; sim+analytic agree to <0.3%, all 4 pre-regs confirmed)**
c7 is the BATCH curse (draw K, ship the max-observed, inflated by E[max_K]). But a real agent searches SEQUENTIALLY: generate candidates until one looks "good enough" (observed > threshold τ), ship it. The shipped result is cursed by TRUNCATION — you stopped BECAUSE it looked good (an up-fluctuation). Modeled it: true q~N(0,1), observed=q+N(0,σ²), σ=0.6; stop at first observed>τ; held-out re-eval = a fresh independent observation (`runs/cycle157_sequential_stopping_curse/seq_stop.py`→`score.json`, 20k reps). **(1) P1 CONFIRMED — the stopped candidate is INFLATED (observed>true) and the ABSOLUTE bias GROWS with τ: bias=observed−true = 0.336 (τ=0.5) → 0.433 → 0.539 → 0.653 → 0.766 (τ=2.5). (2) P2 CONFIRMED + ANALYTIC-matched — higher τ gives a genuinely BETTER result (true quality 0.939→1.207→1.510→1.828→2.154, you searched longer) BUT the inflation FACTOR observed/true is CONSTANT at 1.357 ≈ the closed form 1+σ²=1.360 (to <0.3% at every τ). So raising the bar trades better-true-quality against larger-absolute-overstatement — the calibration RATIO (bias/true=σ²) is fixed, but the absolute gap grows with the bar. (3) P3 CONFIRMED — a HELD-OUT re-eval of the stopped candidate ≈ true at EVERY τ (held-out 0.939/1.212/1.511/1.833/2.155 vs true 0.939/1.207/1.510/1.828/2.154, within CI; held−true≈0): the SAME fix as c7/c45 removes the truncation inflation entirely, in this new setting. (4) P4 CONFIRMED — the search COST explodes with τ: mean candidates-until-stop = 3.0 → 5.1 → 10.1 → 23.1 → 62.6 (super-linear, the tail of N(0,1+σ²)).** **Delta — a NEW selection mechanism that generalizes the loop's centerpiece to the REALISTIC agent workflow (sequential "good enough" stopping, not batch best-of-K): an agent that ships the first candidate clearing its internal quality bar τ is systematically over-confident by a CONSTANT FACTOR (1+σ², the noise-to-total-variance ratio = 1.36 here), and the absolute over-statement GROWS as it raises its bar (a stricter agent finds a better result but over-states it MORE, and pays an exploding search cost). This is DISTINCT from c7's batch curse — different knob (the threshold τ, not the count K), different geometry (threshold-TRUNCATION bias, not the max-order-statistic), different scaling (constant factor in τ vs E[max_K]~√(2lnK) in K) — but the SAME underlying cause (selection on observed → observed>true) and the SAME fix (an independent held-out re-eval, unbiased). So the centerpiece holds across BOTH real workflows: whether you batch-and-select (c7) or sequentially-stop-at-good-enough (c157), the shipped result is inflated and only an independent re-eval tells you its true quality. DECISION-RELEVANT (a parallel-agent lab): agents mostly DON'T batch-then-rank — they stop at "good enough" — so the sequential-stop curse is the one that actually bites in deployment; budget for it (the shipped result is ~(1+σ²)× over-stated, more in absolute terms the higher the bar), and re-evaluate every shipped result on a held-out it didn't stop on. Composes c7/c57 (the curse + closed form) into the sequential-stopping setting.** **Confidence: HIGH (sim+ANALYTIC agreement — the inflation factor matches 1+σ² to <0.3% at every τ, the held-out unbiasedness is exact, like the centerpiece c7/c57; 20k reps, tight CIs). All 4 pre-regs confirmed (none refuted — a clean, well-specified mechanism). HONEST (meth-23): the truncation/selection bias is textbook (conditioning on observed>τ inflates the estimate — the same family as the winner's curse / regression-to-the-mean / publication bias) — FRONTIER-RECOVERABLE; the value is (a) instantiating it in the SEQUENTIAL-STOPPING workflow that agents actually use (the loop's prior curse findings were all batch/best-of-K — this is the realistic complement), (b) the clean result that the inflation FACTOR is constant (1+σ²) while the absolute bias grows with the bar, and (c) tying it to the same held-out fix (the centerpiece generalizes). Caveats: iid Gaussian candidates (real candidates are correlated / non-stationary — a drifting generator changes the truncation); a FIXED known τ (a real agent's "good enough" bar is fuzzy/adaptive); σ known (the inflation factor 1+σ² requires knowing the noise variance — itself estimated from a held-out, c132's one-level-down point); ships the FIRST over-τ (not the best-so-far — a "keep searching for better" agent is a hybrid of c7 and c157). The durable claims (sequential-stop ships an inflated result; inflation factor ≈1+σ² constant in τ; absolute bias + search cost grow with the bar; the held-out re-eval is the unbiased fix) hold.**

### [2026-06-14] Real agents APPLY the c149 breadth-vs-depth rule — but the VERIFIER-GATE is the subtle, capability-sensitive half: Opus weights it (3/3, "when the selector is broken, breadth degenerates into pick-at-random"), Haiku misses it (S3 PARALLEL, over-indexes on coverage) — validates c149 behaviorally + ties c152/c75 (real-agent; high-α) — **keep, with a meth-5 caveat (my "barely-better-than-chance" S3 left a residual-signal loophole); the capability SPLIT on the gate is the clean result**
c149's rule: at fixed compute, PARALLELIZE (N short attempts + select best) pays IFF (a) attempt-ceilings VARY *and* (b) the verifier/selector is good; else run ONE LONG agent. The subtle half (b, the c149/c152 insight) is that breadth buys COVERAGE but a noisy verifier converts coverage into the winner's curse. #10d: does a real agent apply this — especially the gate? Pre-built 3 scenarios (meth-24, known c149-correct answer), same fixed-compute breadth-vs-depth choice, Haiku+Opus (`runs/cycle156_parallelize_choice/`, `score.json`). **(1) BOTH models correct on the two intuitive cases: S1 (approaches VARY widely + reliable evaluator) → BOTH PARALLEL (Opus: "you ship the max not the mean — the max of 20 tail-exposed draws beats one heavily-refined mediocre draw"); S2 (approaches reach SIMILAR quality + reliable evaluator) → BOTH DEPTH (Opus: "low potential-spread, no diversity bonus left on the table; refinement converts undercooked into at-ceiling"). So agents apply the coverage-vs-refinement logic (the ceiling-variance factor) correctly. (2) THE SPLIT on S3 — the VERIFIER-GATE (approaches VARY widely BUT the evaluator is ~random/gameable): OPUS → DEPTH (3/3 vs c149), explicitly weighting the gate: "PARALLEL throws away its one advantage to a near-random/gameable selector... when the selector is broken, breadth-then-select degenerates into 'pick at random and barely develop it'. Depth dominates. The only world where PARALLEL wins is if the metric were trustworthy enough to surface the gem — the premise removes that." HAIKU → PARALLEL (2/3 vs c149), UNDER-weighting the gate: "even a barely-better-than-chance metric correlates with SOME quality signal... selection finds them" + "deep refinement risks overfitting to the noisy metric." So the more capable model reasons about the SELECTION step (can't realize coverage without a selector — the c149/c152 insight); the weaker one over-indexes on GENERATION/coverage.** **Delta — validates c149 behaviorally AND localizes the subtle half: capable agents apply the breadth-vs-depth rule (ceiling-variance → parallel-vs-depth) on the intuitive cases, but the VERIFIER-GATE (a broken selector negates breadth's coverage) is the SUBTLE, capability-sensitive half — Opus catches it (depth when the selector is random), Haiku misses it (parallelizes on residual coverage). This is the SAME pattern as c75 (capability separates at the HARD boundary, not the easy cases) and c127 (capability doesn't help on the subtle case — but here it DOES, on this particular gate), and it confirms c152's framing (selection is verifier-gated; the gate is the non-obvious half that gets under-weighted). DECISION-RELEVANT: when teaching/prompting an agent fleet to allocate compute, the breadth-vs-depth-by-ceiling-variance part is reliably reasoned, but the verifier-gate (don't parallelize if you can't trust the selector) must be made EXPLICIT for a weaker model — it defaults to "more attempts = more coverage" and forgets it can't pick the winner. Ties the c149 fleet rule to the c98 "agents reconstruct the playbook" thread: they reconstruct the COVERAGE half easily, the SELECTION-gate half unreliably (capability-dependent).** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=1/scenario/model, same Claude family Haiku+Opus → not independent; Q5 the low-α check). The S1/S2 agreement is clean (both models, decisive reasoning); the S3 SPLIT is the result (Opus weights the gate, Haiku doesn't) and the reasoning is quoted, not inferred. ⚠️ HONEST meth-5 CAVEAT (my setup): S3 said the evaluator is "barely better than chance," NOT exactly random — so a tiny residual signal × huge ceiling-variance leaves a DEFENSIBLE argument for PARALLEL (Haiku took it: "barely-better-than-chance correlates with some quality"). So Haiku's S3 is the less-c149-aligned reading, not a clear error; Opus read the spirit ("selector broken → pick-at-random → depth"), Haiku the letter ("residual signal → exploit it"). An EXACTLY-random/adversarial selector would make DEPTH unambiguous (→ a cleaner re-test). The substantive claim survives the caveat: the verifier-gate is the subtle half, and reading it correctly (a near-random selector negates breadth) is capability/care-sensitive. Caveats: n=1/cell, high-α, 3 scenarios, my framings; the S3 ambiguity (the loophole). The durable claim (agents apply the ceiling-variance breadth-vs-depth rule; the verifier-gate is the subtle, capability-sensitive half that a weaker model under-weights) holds.**

### [2026-06-14] Verification LATENCY (NEW mechanism, tail/checkpoint pillar — the temporal dimension c82 omitted): DON'T check faster than your verifier's latency — the optimal check-interval is m*≈max(c82-optimal, ~2D); checking more often can't beat the latency floor, so latency is a modest irreducible tax fixed by a FASTER verifier (lower D) or BETTER generation (lower p), not more checks — **keep, with 2 honest pre-reg corrections (m* RISES for large D, not constant; the tax is 16× smaller than naive p·T·D); clean MC, mechanism cross-checked**
c82 ("checkpoint THROUGHOUT, evenly") and c92 (long-horizon leaderboard) both assumed INSTANT verification. Real checks take TIME: a check started at step t completes at t+D (latency D) while the agent keeps generating on a possibly-undetected error. NEW question: how does D change the optimal check-interval m* and the cost? Modeled c82's propagation (state CLEAN or CORRUPT-since-e; while clean, error w.p. p; a check every m steps completes at +D and catches/rolls-back any uncaught error ≤ its fire time; wasted = corrupt-state steps; cost = wasted + n_checks·c) (`runs/cycle155_verification_latency/latency.py`→`score.json`, T=4000, p=0.02, c=20, 400 reps). **(1) P1 PARTIALLY REFUTED → sharper rule: m*(D) is FLAT for small D (m*=80 at D=0,10,25) but RISES for large D (120 at D=50, 200 at D=100) — roughly m*≈max(c82-optimal, ~2D). My additive-delay analytic (detection delay = m/2 + D → m* independent of D) was right for D≲m* but WRONG for large D: once D exceeds the interval, checking faster cannot cut the detection time (it's floored at D), so the only effect of small m is its check-cost → m* rises to save checks. THE RULE: don't check faster than your verification latency. (2) P2 CONFIRMED in direction, magnitude over-predicted: cost@m* rises ~LINEARLY in D (2990→3060→3203→3324→3502 for D=0→100; slope 5.04), an irreducible latency tax — but FAR below the naive p·T·D (predicted slope 80, actual 5.04, 16× smaller) because propagation SELF-LIMITS the episode count (no new error starts while already corrupt → far fewer than p·T=80 episodes incur the +D delay) AND m* adapts. (3) P3 CONFIRMED: checking MORE often is actively counterproductive — m=5 costs 16156 vs 2990 at m*=80 (D=0), dominated by check cost (800 checks×20=16000), and stays ~5× worse at every D; so the lever against latency is NOT smaller m — it's LOWER D (faster verifier) or LOWER p (better generation, c118).** **Delta — adds the TEMPORAL dimension to the loop's checkpoint findings (c82/c92 assumed instant checks): (a) the optimal check-interval is m*≈max(c82's √(2c/p)-type optimum, ~2D) — DON'T verify faster than your verifier's latency, because below the latency floor extra checks cost without catching errors any sooner (detection is floored at D); this is a concrete, counterintuitive scheduling rule (you might think slow verification → check more often to compensate; actually check LESS often, ~at the latency, and accept the tax). (b) Latency is an IRREDUCIBLE cost tax (linear in D) that frequent checking CANNOT reduce — but it's MODEST (propagation self-limits the episodes that pay it), and the only real levers are a FASTER verifier (lower D) or BETTER generation (fewer errors to catch late, lower p — the c118 generator-ceiling lever reappears). DECISION-RELEVANT for a parallel-agent lab with SLOW verification (expensive LLM-judge, human review, or a long re-eval): set the checkpoint interval to ~the verification latency, not arbitrarily small; budget for the latency tax; and invest in verifier SPEED, not just verifier accuracy — a fast-but-rougher checker that catches errors PROMPTLY can beat a slow-but-precise one that catches them late (a new axis beyond c45 independence / c93 recall: verifier LATENCY). Composes c82 (checkpoint evenly) × c118 (generation lever) with the realistic non-instant-verification constraint.** **Confidence: clean MC (400 reps, tight CIs; the mechanism is transparent — check-cost (T/m)·c dominates small m, latency floors detection at D; cross-checked by hand: m=5 → 800 checks × c=20 = 16000 check-cost matches the 16156 total). The 2 pre-reg corrections (m* rises for large D; tax 16× below naive) are measure-before-keep working (my additive-delay analytic was a stylized approximation that the MC corrected). HONEST (meth-23): the QUALITATIVE rule (don't check faster than latency; latency is a tax fixed by speed not frequency) is intuitive once stated; the value is making the temporal dimension EXPLICIT (the loop's checkpoint findings silently assumed instant checks), the QUANTIFIED m*≈max(c82, ~2D) shape, and the honest correction that the tax is modest (self-limited by propagation). Caveats: stylized (fixed per-step p, fixed latency D, propagation = total-downstream-corruption, c=20 sets the m* level so the exact 80/120/200 are c/p-specific — the DURABLE claim is the SHAPE: flat-then-rising-~2D); the rollback model re-does the corrupt span without re-erroring during the MC's accounting (counts corrupt-time, a clean proxy for wasted work); a partially-corrupting error (not total propagation) would soften the tax. The durable claims (m*≈max(c82-optimal, ~2D) — don't check faster than latency; latency is a modest irreducible tax fixed by faster verification or better generation, not more checks) hold.**

### [2026-06-14] c94's D*∝1/p generalizes to HETEROGENEOUS branches as a PER-BRANCH rule (d_i*∝1/p_i): under a fixed depth budget, allocate depth toward the RELIABLE (low-p) branches — adaptive beats uniform, the gain GROWS with branch-heterogeneity (2.3× fleet quality at strong spread) (HARDEN c94; scaffold pillar) — **keep (extends the aspect-ratio rule to realistic heterogeneous fleets; clean sim, all 3 pre-regs confirmed, d*∝1/p analytically verified)**
c94 found a scaffold's optimal depth D*∝1/p (the per-step error rate) — but assumed a SINGLE p (all parallel branches equally risky). #10a HARDENS it for HETEROGENEOUS branches (real approaches differ in reliability): under a FIXED total depth budget B=Σd_i across W branches, allocate depth UNIFORMLY or ADAPTIVELY (deeper on low-p branches)? Modeled c94's refinement-vs-propagation faithfully: a branch refined to depth d under error p has quality Q(d,p)=d·(1−p)^d (refinement d × survival-without-corruption (1−p)^d); fleet selects argmax(Q+noise), realizes true Q (`runs/cycle154_heterogeneous_depth/het_depth.py`→`score.json`, W=8, B=80, 20k reps). **(1) P3 CONFIRMED — the per-branch optimal depth d*(p)=argmax_d d·(1−p)^d = −1/ln(1−p) ≈ 1/p: d*(0.10)=9.49 (1/p=10), d*(0.30)=2.80 (3.33), d*(0.015)=66.2 (66.7) — so c94's D*∝1/p becomes a PER-BRANCH rule d_i*∝1/p_i. (2) P1 CONTROL CONFIRMED — HOMOGENEOUS p (all 0.10) → adaptive == uniform EXACTLY (both 3.487, gain +0.000; with equal p, ∝1/p_i IS uniform — no heterogeneity to exploit). (3) P2 CONFIRMED — under HETEROGENEOUS p, ADAPTIVE (d_i∝1/p_i) BEATS UNIFORM and the gain GROWS with the p-spread: adaptive−uniform = +0.60 (mild, p∈[.06,.16]) → +4.30 (moderate, [.03,.30]) → +11.27 (strong, [.015,.50]); at strong heterogeneity adaptive 19.76 vs uniform 8.49 = a 2.3× fleet-quality improvement. The mechanism: uniform OVER-refines the risky (high-p) branches (where extra depth just corrupts, (1−p)^d collapses) and UNDER-refines the reliable (low-p) ones (whose quality keeps climbing toward a high d*). (4) the simple ∝1/p_i heuristic captures 71–90% of the ORACLE's gain over uniform (mild 83%, moderate 90%, strong 71%); the oracle (greedy water-fill) concentrates EVEN MORE on the reliable branches at strong heterogeneity (the 1/p heuristic under-concentrates there — it still spreads some budget onto near-hopeless high-p branches).** **Delta — extends c94's aspect-ratio rule from a single-p scaffold to the realistic HETEROGENEOUS-fleet case, with a concrete depth-allocation rule: when your parallel approaches differ in reliability (different per-step error rates p_i), do NOT refine them equally — allocate depth toward the RELIABLE branches (∝1/p_i as a good heuristic, or concentrate even harder per the oracle), and don't waste depth deep-refining a risky branch where each step just corrupts it. c94's D*∝1/p holds PER-BRANCH (d_i*∝1/p_i), and the VALUE of adaptive allocation grows with how heterogeneous the fleet is (negligible when branches are similar, 2.3× when they span [.015,.50]). For a parallel-agent lab: estimate each approach's reliability and give the reliable ones more refinement budget — uniform depth wastes compute corrupting the risky branches. This composes with c149 (the FLEET-level breadth-vs-depth = coverage-vs-selection): #10a is the within-budget DEPTH-allocation across heterogeneous branches, c149 is the breadth-vs-depth split — together, allocate breadth to coverage (if ceilings vary) and depth to the reliable branches (if reliabilities vary).** **Confidence: clean (20k-rep sim; the d*∝1/p is the ANALYTIC argmax, verified to the decimal; controls fire — homogeneous→adaptive==uniform exactly, oracle≥adaptive≥uniform everywhere; the homogeneous Q=10·0.9¹⁰=3.487 matches by hand). All 3 pre-regs confirmed. HONEST (meth-23): this is a HARDEN of c94 (not a new mechanism) — the direction (concentrate on reliable branches) is intuitive once you see each branch has its own d*∝1/p; the value is the QUANTIFIED gain-grows-with-heterogeneity (2.3× at strong spread) + the per-branch d_i*∝1/p_i confirmation + the honest oracle-gap (the 1/p heuristic leaves 10–29% on the table, under-concentrating at strong heterogeneity). Caveats: the Q(d,p)=d·(1−p)^d model is c94's stylized refinement×propagation (real refinement curves differ — but the per-branch-optimal-then-allocate logic is model-general); fixed known p_i (real p_i must be ESTIMATED, and mis-estimating a branch's reliability would mis-allocate — an adaptive scheme should re-estimate p_i as it refines, untested here); the selection noise σ_obs=0.5 mainly sets the level not the ordering. The durable claims (d_i*∝1/p_i per-branch; adaptive depth-allocation toward reliable branches beats uniform; the gain grows with heterogeneity) hold.**

### [2026-06-14] The winner's-curse lens turned on the loop's OWN self-edits (the spine, meth-6 applied reflexively): the loop's self-improvement is NOT harmfully cursed, but it runs an UN-NAMED two-type validation asymmetry — RATE/capability edits get measure-before-keep (where the curse risk lives), GUARD edits get followed-by-use (a metric rise is the wrong test) — and conflating them is the trap — **keep, and it FED a self-edit (meth-26 names the rule); a real retrospective audit of all 27 self-edits, counts cited**
The loop's founding spine is "how do I measure whether my own self-edits improve me?" (= RE-Bench's open-ended-progress problem). #22 turns the loop's OWN centerpiece (the winner's curse: selecting by a noisy metric overstates value, c7; applied reflexively in meth-6) onto its self-edits: is KEEPING them a selection artifact? Retrospective audit of the full CHANGELOG (`runs/cycle153_selfedit_curse_audit/score.json`, classifying every edit by type + validation status, counts from a programmatic scan this cycle). **(1) THE COUNTS: 27 self-edits (v2/v3 + meth-2..25). 11 (41%) have a recorded MEASURED-positive outcome (HELPED/CONFIRMED/SUPPORTED — v2/meth-2/3/4/7/11/12/18/21/22/23); 2 formal ROLLBACK events (rollbacks_cum=2: meth-11 corrected meth-4/meth-10's data-only-LLM-collider-awareness over-claim; meth-14 self-corrected its 'residual optimism' direction) + 1 within-thread caveat correction (meth-20@c66) — the discipline VISIBLY worked (re-testing overturned real edits); 14 (52%) kept by FOLLOWED-BY-USE with no dedicated outcome cycle. (2) THE TYPE SPLIT (the key): the 27 edits are 14 RATE/CAPABILITY/verification-LADDER (a testable prediction → measure-before-keep is the right test) + 10 GUARD/anti-overclaim (meth-5/6/15/21/22/23/24/25, v3, meth-14 — honesty/claim-space constraints) + 3 META-METRIC. Among the 14 capability edits, 8 (57%) were MEASURED-or-rolled-back — measurement CONCENTRATED exactly where the winner's-curse risk lives. Among the 10 GUARD edits, only 3 were spot-checked (meth-21/22/23 measured-HELPED) and 7 kept by followed-by-use — because a GUARD's validation is 'did it prevent its error class?', NOT a metric rise. (3) THE AGGREGATE CROSS-CHECK is CONSISTENT: the c100/c120/c140 audits found the findings-rate FLAT (~0.80) — and that is NOT evidence the kept edits failed, because the 52% kept-by-use are GUARDS + ladder-rules that DON'T target the rate (meth-15 explicitly encodes 'rigor not rate' = the loop pre-emptively REFUSING the rate-improvement over-claim = meth-6's winner's-curse-on-self humility already working).** **Delta — the spine question answered honestly, and it FED a self-edit (meth-26): the loop's self-improvement is NOT harmfully winner's-cursed — measurement is concentrated where the curse risk actually lives (capability edits: 57% measured-or-rolled, incl. 2 real rollbacks), and the 52% kept-without-measurement are predominantly GUARDS whose valid test is error-prevention not a metric. BUT the audit exposes an UN-NAMED ASYMMETRY: the loop states 'measure-before-keep' as ONE uniform rule (constitution §6c) yet in practice applies TWO different validations by edit-type — and never named the distinction. That risks (a) MIS-READING its own rigor (an outside reader sees '52% kept without measurement' and cries insufficient-rigor, OR sees '2 rollbacks in 27' and cries insufficient-skepticism — when really there are two valid bases), and (b) the ACTUAL winner's-curse failure mode: a future RATE/capability edit kept on followed-by-use (unmeasured) — which is exactly the curse (keeping an edit selected by a noisy good-feeling). So the fix (meth-26, encoded this cycle): CLASSIFY each self-edit as RATE/CAPABILITY (→ measure-before-keep on the metric; NEVER keep one unmeasured — the curse lives here) vs GUARD/CONSTRAIN (→ validate by FOLLOWED-BY-USE + did-it-prevent-its-error-class; a metric rise is the WRONG test, flat-rate ≠ failure). This SHARPENS meth-6 + §6c (clarifies what 'measure' means per type) without contradicting them. Transferable lesson for any self-improving agent: a self-improving system's edits are NOT homogeneous; applying metric-validation to a guard makes it look like a failure, and applying use-validation to a capability edit IS the winner's curse — classify and match.** **Confidence: the COUNTS are exact (programmatic scan of CHANGELOG.md this cycle, cited in score.json: 27 edits / 11 measured / 2 rollback-events / 14 by-use; the type-split is my classification, defensible per the edit rationales). The interpretive claim (two valid validation bases, the loop applies the right one by type) is a reasoned judgment, not a measurement — I could be charitable to the loop here (self-audit = high-α, the meth-21 trap applied to THIS finding: I designed+ran+interpreted my own self-audit). HONEST (meth-21 self-applied): this IS a high-α self-assessment — an independent reviewer (the operator) should spot-check whether the 7 'kept-by-use' guards genuinely prevented their error classes or are just unfalsifiable; the winner's-curse lens could be applied MORE harshly (a skeptic would say '52% unmeasured = the loop can't tell if half its self-edits help'). The honest floor: the loop measured where it mattered (capability, 2 rollbacks) and the rate-flat audit is consistent with the guards-don't-target-rate reading — but 'the guards genuinely help' rests on followed-by-use, which is weaker than measurement. Caveats: n=27 edits (small); meth-19a/meth-20-caveats are refinements I counted as part of their parent (a judgment); the type-classification has borderline cases (meth-9/13 meta-metric vs guard). The durable claim (self-edits split into measure-before-keep RATE edits and followed-by-use GUARD edits; the loop concentrated measurement on the curse-prone type; name the distinction = meth-26) holds.**

### [2026-06-14] GROUND of c119 (generator diversity): the literature INDEPENDENTLY confirms the coverage half ("diverse prompts after Best-of-N → significantly lower error rates") AND names a "diversity-fidelity trade-off" (= c119's wild-vs-reliable) — but it REFRAMES c119's "wild is easier to verify": diversity helps ONLY with an accurate verifier (selection quality is verifier-gated, ties c149) — **keep (a partial independent check on the under-grounded generation pillar; P1/P3 confirmed abstract-verified, P2 grounded-with-a-refining-caveat; positions the loop's quantitative value-add)**
c119 (generator VARIANCE ∝ fleet size: a WILD/high-variance generator beats a RELIABLE one at large fleet K, AND is counterintuitively EASIER to verify — its tail candidate stands out → smaller winner's curse) was entirely loop-sim; c134 grounded c118 but explicitly deferred c119's diversity claim. Grounded it via WebSearch+WebFetch (independent of the loop's Claude model) against the sampling-diversity / best-of-N literature. **PRE-REG (before searching): P1 (diversity→coverage at scale) strongly confirmed; P2 (wild=easier-to-verify) mostly silent/loop's-value-add; P3 (the reliable→wild flip) partial. RESULTS: (1) P1 CONFIRMED (abstract-verified, arXiv:2502.11027 "Diversified Sampling Improves Scaling LLM Inference", Wang et al.): "responses generated from diverse prompts after Best-of-N selection exhibit significantly LOWER ERROR RATES than those produced from stationary prompts" — i.e. a diverse/wild generator beats a uniform/clustered ("stationary") one under best-of-N, exactly c119's wild-beats-reliable direction, and matches c119's MECHANISM intuition (the reliable generator's clustered/"stationary" candidates are the problem). (2) P3 CONFIRMED-IN-NAME (abstract-verified): the same paper "derive[s] a DIVERSITY-FIDELITY TRADE-OFF principle that guides the design of sampling strategies" — that IS c119's wild(=diversity)-vs-reliable(=fidelity/high-mean) tradeoff, independently named in the literature (BUT the abstract does not detail the trade-off's shape, so it grounds the EXISTENCE/direction, not c119's specific quantitative flip-K≈30-50). (3) P2 REFRAMED (the interesting one): the best-of-N-selection literature (generative verifiers / GenSelect / self-certainty, search-level) does NOT confirm c119's clean "wild is easier to verify"; it says diversity helps ONLY when the VERIFIER is accurate — "high pairwise accuracy does not necessarily translate into effective Best-of-N selection," failure modes are "the brittleness of scoring mechanisms," and improving the verifier (discriminative→generative RM) lifts GSM8K best-of-N 73%→93.4%. So selection quality is VERIFIER-GATED.** **Delta — c119's generation pillar is now PARTIALLY GROUNDED (6th external grounding after Deflated-Sharpe/RE-Bench/Snell/Recht/Monkeys/saturation), with one half confirmed and one half REFRAMED: (a) the COVERAGE half (a wild/diverse generator beats a reliable/clustered one under best-of-N scaling, and there's a diversity-fidelity tradeoff) is independently real (arXiv:2502.11027, abstract-verified) — so c119 is NOT a loop-sim artifact. (b) BUT c119's clean sub-claim "wild is EASIER to verify (its tail stands out → smaller curse)" is REFRAMED by the literature: diversity's benefit is VERIFIER-GATED — a wild generator also produces wild BAD candidates, and a brittle verifier can be fooled by them, so diversity helps selection ONLY with an accurate verifier. This is actually the loop's OWN c149 result arriving from the literature (verifier quality gates the coverage payoff) and it REFINES c119: "wild beats reliable AND is easier to select" holds when the verifier is good enough that the spread-out best candidate's larger #1-vs-#2 gap dominates the eval-noise — but a brittle verifier negates it (the c119 'heavy-left-tail' caveat, made concrete). So the corrected rule: a wild generator at scale is doubly-advantaged ONLY GIVEN A GOOD VERIFIER (it raises the ceiling AND, with a competent selector, the best stands out); pair high diversity with strong verification — they compound (c81), but the verifier is the gate (c149). The loop's value-add beyond the literature: the QUANTITATIVE flip-K (matched to the order-statistic closed form, E[max_K]=2.0→K≈30) and the specific winner's-curse mechanism (the reliable generator's clustered candidates have the BIGGER curse gap, c90 formula gap∝σ_obs²/√(σ_gen²+σ_obs²)) — the literature names the diversity-fidelity tradeoff QUALITATIVELY; the loop quantifies it and ties it to the curse.** **Confidence: the two LOAD-BEARING anchors are ABSTRACT-VERIFIED (WebFetch of arXiv:2502.11027: the "lower error rates after Best-of-N from diverse prompts" + the "diversity-fidelity trade-off principle" quotes are verbatim from the abstract). The P2-reframe (verifier-gated selection) is SEARCH-LEVEL (generative-verifier / best-of-N literature summaries, not a single primary paper fetched this cycle) — directionally solid (it's the well-known "verifier quality matters for best-of-N" point + the loop's own c149) but treat it as commentary-grounded, not primary-verified. This is a PARTIAL independent check (literature ⊥ the loop's model) on c119's DIRECTION (diversity helps best-of-N, the tradeoff exists), NOT its magnitudes (the flip-K and the curse-gap-ratio are loop-sim-specific). HONEST (meth-23): my pre-reg was mostly right (P1 strong, P2 partial) but P3 was BETTER grounded than predicted (the paper independently NAMES the diversity-fidelity tradeoff) and P2 was not just "silent" but actively REFRAMED (verifier-gated) — a useful correction that ties c119 to c149. The durable update: c119's coverage/tradeoff half is literature-real; its "easier to verify" half is verifier-conditional (the loop's c149 gate), and the loop's quantification (flip-K, curse-gap) is the value-add the literature states only qualitatively. Caveats: 2502.11027 grounds the diverse-beats-stationary DIRECTION not the reliable-vs-wild Gaussian flip specifically; P2 is commentary-grounded; one paper + search-level secondary sources (not an exhaustive survey).**

### [2026-06-14] The loop's centerpiece FIX works on a real agent: given a 2nd INDEPENDENT draw, a capable agent SEPARATES real-from-lucky that it CANNOT separate from one observation (closes the c131/132 arc; real-agent) — corr(est,true) 0.27→1.00, real-vs-lucky separation 0.05→1.35 (true gap 1.50), both models — **keep (validates the loop's central fix BEHAVIORALLY, not just the problem; high-α n=2 models, decisive + mechanism reasoning-confirmed)**
c131 showed a real agent KNOWS the winner's curse (discounts a selected strategy's Sharpe spontaneously) but over-shrinks to a generic prior; c132 proved even the OPTIMAL estimate can't separate a genuine winner from a lucky fake from ONE observation — the info isn't there; the loop's fix (c7) is a SECOND INDEPENDENT draw (a fresh held-out re-eval). #3c tests whether a real agent USES it. Pre-built 5 strategies with KNOWN true forward Sharpe (meth-24); the KEY pair: `alpha-momentum` (true 1.8) and `vol-carry` (true 0.3, a lucky fake) have NEARLY IDENTICAL backtests (2.1 vs 2.0) but OOS 1.7 vs 0.2 — indistinguishable from the backtest alone, separable with the 2nd draw. Two arms, Haiku+Opus (`runs/cycle151_second_draw_separation/`, `score.json`). **(1) P1 CONFIRMED — ARM A (backtest only, = c131) CANNOT separate: both models give the real winner and the lucky fake near-identical estimates (Haiku alpha-momentum 0.85 vs vol-carry 0.80; Opus 0.90 vs 0.85 — separation 0.05 vs the true gap 1.50), all shrunk to a generic ~0.25–0.9 prior; corr(estimate, true) = 0.27 (Haiku) / 0.32 (Opus) — barely better than noise (replicates c131's blind over-shrink). (2) P2 CONFIRMED — ARM B (backtest + a fresh independent OOS, = the FIX) SEPARATES: both models give the real winner ~1.5 (HIGH, ≈ its 1.7 OOS) and the lucky fake ~0.1–0.2 (LOW, ≈ its 0.2 OOS); corr(estimate, true) JUMPS to 0.999 (Haiku) / 1.000 (Opus), and the real-vs-lucky separation jumps to 1.40 / 1.30 (recovering ~90% of the true 1.50 gap). (3) P3 CONFIRMED — the mechanism is reasoning-explicit and CORRECT: the agents do NOT naively average (a (2.0+0.2)/2 averager would put the lucky vol-carry at ~1.1) — they correctly treat backtest≫OOS as evidence of OVERFITTING MAGNITUDE, not signal. Opus: "where backtest ≫ OOS, the backtest is informative only as evidence of how much overfitting happened — it does NOT raise the estimate; where they agree, the estimate sits just below OOS"; Haiku: "OOS dead at exactly zero = strong signal this is a pure selection artifact."** **Delta — CLOSES the c131/132 centerpiece-on-a-real-agent arc by validating the FIX (not just the problem): c131 (the agent knows the curse but over-shrinks blindly) → c132 (the limit is INFORMATIONAL — even optimal can't separate from one observation, needs a 2nd draw) → c151 (given the 2nd independent draw, the agent USES it and separates near-perfectly, corr ~0→1.0). So the loop's whole thesis is now enacted on a real agent end-to-end: getting an answer is easy, knowing it's real is hard FROM ONE LOOK, and the way you resolve it is an INDEPENDENT re-evaluation (c7/c45) — and a capable agent correctly trusts the independent draw over the inflated in-sample number when handed it. This is the behavioral complement to c98/c99 (the agent can DERIVE the curse) — here it correctly USES the fix's input. DECISION-RELEVANT: a verification layer that supplies an INDEPENDENT held-out re-eval is not just theoretically unbiased (c7) — a real agent acts on it correctly (trusts OOS over backtest, doesn't average), so the fix is deployable; the binding constraint is SUPPLYING the independent draw (the executed held-out, c99's moat), not the agent's ability to use it.** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=2 models, same Claude family Haiku+Opus, n=1 trajectory per cell → not independent; Q5 the low-α different-model check). The result is DECISIVE and consistent across both models (corr 0.3→1.0, separation 0.05→1.35), and the MECHANISM is reasoning-confirmed (both models explicitly weight OOS over backtest as overfitting-evidence, the non-trivial correct behavior — they don't average). HONEST (meth-23): the scenario is meth-24 hand-built — I chose OOS values that ARE discriminating (real high-on-both, lucky low-OOS), so this tests whether the agent USES a discriminating 2nd draw, NOT whether real OOS data is always discriminating (that's the realism/Q5 question — a real fresh OOS Sharpe is itself noisy, c62/c114, so the separation would be less clean on real data). The finding is "the agent correctly trusts an independent draw over an inflated in-sample number" — the behavioral validation of c7's fix — not "real OOS always separates." Caveats: high-α; n=1/cell; the agent may be pattern-matching "backtest≫OOS=overfit" as a learned heuristic rather than deriving it (fine — it USES it correctly either way); Arm B is "easier" by construction (the OOS is near-truth) — but the non-trivial result is that the agent TRUSTS it over the high backtest rather than anchoring/averaging. The durable claim (a capable agent uses an independent 2nd draw to separate real-from-lucky, correctly weighting it over the inflated in-sample score — so the c7 held-out fix transfers behaviorally) holds across both models.**

### [2026-06-14] Parallel-vs-sequential compute allocation for an agent lab IS the coverage-vs-selection tradeoff (SYNTHESIS c118×c7×c11; scaffold pillar): BREADTH (N parallel agents, select best) buys coverage of the ceiling distribution but pays the winner's-curse selection tax; parallelism only pays when attempt-ceilings VARY *and* the verifier is good — else run one long agent — **keep (a novel unification of 3 loop threads into the parallel-agent-lab product question; sim cross-checked EXACTLY vs the analytic E[max_N]·frac; P1/P2/P4 confirmed, P3 partially)**
Diversified off benchmark-design (per the c148 note) to the thinnest pillar (scaffold). The parallel-agent-lab question: at FIXED compute C, run N SHORT parallel agents and SELECT the best (BREADTH), or 1 LONG agent (DEPTH)? Modeled it as coverage-vs-selection (`runs/cycle149_parallel_vs_sequential/breadth_depth.py` → `score.json`; attempt ceiling q~N(1,σ_ceil), refinement frac=1−e^(−t/τ) at t=C/N steps, τ=10, C=100; breadth selects argmax of realized+N(0,σ_v) noisy-verifier score; 20k reps; swept σ_ceil∈{0,.1,.3,.6}×σ_v∈{0,.2,.5}). **(1) P1 CONTROL CONFIRMED — σ_ceil=0 (identical ceilings) → N*=1 (DEPTH) at every σ_v; the breadth curve DECLINES monotonically with N (1.000→0.095) because with no ceiling-variance, parallelism just under-refines each attempt for zero coverage benefit. (2) P2 CONFIRMED — high σ_ceil + low σ_v → parallelism pays big: σ_ceil=0.6,σ_v=0 → N*=4, realized 1.487 vs depth 0.996 (+49%); σ_ceil=0.3 → N*=4, +0.20. (3) P3 PARTIAL — the selection tax LOWERS N* in the intermediate regime (σ_ceil=0.3: N* 4→4→2 as σ_v 0→0.2→0.5) but at high σ_ceil=0.6 the coverage gain is large enough that N*=4 survives even σ_v=0.5 (the optimum N is robust when coverage upside dominates the tax) — so the tax caps parallelism but doesn't always collapse it. (4) P4 CONFIRMED — breadth's advantage over depth = the coverage gain, realized ONLY if the verifier can select it: breadth_adv shrinks monotonically with verifier noise at every σ_ceil (σ_ceil=0.6: +0.49→+0.46→+0.33 as σ_v 0→0.2→0.5; σ_ceil=0.1: +0.051→+0.019→+0.002, essentially gone) and grows with σ_ceil (more coverage upside). ADVERSARIAL CROSS-CHECK (exact): breadth realized = E[max of N ceilings]·frac(C/N) — at σ_ceil=0.6,N=4: (1+0.6·E[max₄N(0,1)]=1+0.6·1.029)·(1−e^(−2.5))=1.484 vs measured 1.487; N=2: 1.329 vs 1.327. The model is the coverage formula exactly.** **Delta — a novel UNIFICATION of three loop threads into the parallel-agent-lab compute-allocation rule (high-value for parallel-agent labs): running many parallel agents and selecting the best is BREADTH = buying coverage of the achievable-ceiling distribution (c118: the generator sets the ceiling; sampling N attempts samples N ceilings, advantage ∝σ_ceil·√(2 ln N)) — but the SELECTION is the c7/c90 winner's curse, so a noisy verifier converts coverage into the curse (at σ_v→∞ you pick at random → coverage gain → 0). DEPTH (one long agent) refines a single ceiling fully — no selection tax, but bounded by that one draw. So the rule: (i) parallelism only helps when attempt-CEILINGS VARY on the task (σ_ceil>0) — if all approaches plateau at similar quality, run ONE long agent (N*=1, the control); (ii) N* is INTERIOR (over-parallelizing both under-refines each attempt AND amplifies the selection curse — the breadth curve is single-peaked in N); (iii) VERIFIER QUALITY gates the payoff — the better your selector, the more parallelism pays (the entire breadth advantage is taxed by σ_v). This is the loop's central thesis (coverage is easy, trusted SELECTION is the bottleneck, c118/c134 Monkeys) instantiated as a compute knob: parallelism is a BET ON COVERAGE that only pays if you can VERIFY the winner. Ties c94 (breadth/depth aspect-ratio, here at the FLEET not search-tree level), c7/c90 (the selection curse is the tax), c118/c134 (coverage/ceiling is the prize), c45/c133 (if the parallel agents share a blind spot — correlated ceilings — the coverage benefit shrinks, strengthening "breadth needs genuine diversity").** **Confidence: HIGH on the mechanism (the breadth arm is the analytic coverage formula E[max_N]·frac, cross-checked to 3 decimals; the controls fire — σ_ceil=0→N*=1, monotonic decline; σ_v→∞→random selection→no coverage). P3 was partially refuted (the tax doesn't always lower N* — honest, measure-before-keep). HONEST (meth-23): each ingredient is known (coverage∝√(ln N), the winner's curse, diminishing-returns refinement) — frontier-recoverable; the value-add is the UNIFICATION (parallel-vs-sequential = coverage-vs-selection as ONE compute knob, with the verifier as the gate) + the crisp decision rule for a parallel-agent lab + the exact cross-check. Caveats: the refinement curve (1−e^(−t/τ)) and τ/C are modeling choices — they set the SPECIFIC N* but not the qualitative rule (breadth needs ceiling-variance + a good verifier), which is driven by the coverage-vs-selection structure; INDEPENDENT attempts assumed (real parallel agents on a shared base model have CORRELATED ceilings — c45/c133 — which only SHRINKS the coverage benefit, reinforcing the conclusion); a scalar 'quality' abstraction; no inter-agent communication (a real lab might share partial progress — a hybrid). The durable claims (parallelism pays iff ceilings vary AND the verifier is good; N* is interior; verifier quality gates the payoff; it's coverage-vs-selection) hold across the swept grid.**

### [2026-06-14] Adaptive benchmark design has an OVERFITTING PEAK, and a small calibration set makes you OVER-accept design tweaks while believing the opposite (HARDEN c147 with real signal): true discrimination peaks-then-declines; at A=10 you accept ~2.5× too many tweaks, capture 65% of the achievable gain, yet BELIEVE 4× the truth — a fresh held-out re-eval recovers true quality at any A — **keep, and it REFUTES 2 of my 4 pre-regs (believed-monotonic, R*-grows-with-A → the sharper truth), robustness-checked across 2 signal distributions, cross-checked vs the analytic oracle**
c147 bounded the PURE-NULL adaptive overfit (~√(R/A)); #17c adds real SIGNAL (some design tweaks genuinely help, some hurt) to find the actionable budget: how many adaptive design iterations can a builder afford on ONE calibration set of A agents? Modeled the classic in-sample-vs-out-of-sample overfitting curve in the benchmark-DESIGN setting (`runs/cycle148_design_budget_crossover/design_budget.py` symmetric g_true~N(0,1); `design_budget_sparse.py` sparse-positive mixture, p_signal=0.15; → `score.json`,`score_sparse.json`; D=200 tweaks, σ_cal=σ_obs/√A, REPS=3000). The builder accepts tweaks best-calibration-score-first; BELIEVED quality = Σĝ (in-sample), TRUE = Σg_true (fresh population), R* = argmax TRUE. **(1) P2 CONFIRMED — TRUE discrimination PEAKS then DECLINES (the overfitting curve) at every A, both distributions: the builder eventually sweeps in tweaks whose high calibration score was NOISE (true effect ≤0), degrading real discrimination. (2) P4 CONFIRMED — a FRESH independent held-out re-eval recovers TRUE quality at every A: reeval@R* ≈ true@R* (symmetric 60.66 vs 60.83 at A=10, 79.24 vs 79.24 at A=1000; sparse 20.75 vs 20.59, 30.45 vs 30.47) — the fresh estimate is unbiased for the selected set, so the builder CAN locate the true curve on fresh data. (3) P1 REFUTED — BELIEVED is NOT monotonic; it too peaks (at the count of positive-ĝ tweaks) then declines (I forgot summing sorted-descending values that cross 0 makes a peak). (4) P3 REFUTED, sharper truth — R* (how many to accept) is NOT a budget that grows with A. Under SYMMETRIC signal R*≈D/2 (accept-if-ĝ>0, A-INDEPENDENT); under realistic SPARSE signal R* SHRINKS with A (96→64→43→39→45 for A=10→1000) — at LOW A you grossly OVER-accept because noise makes neutral tweaks look beneficial, and R* falls toward the true beneficial count as A resolves them. (5) THE SELF-DECEPTION (the headline): at A=10 sparse, the builder accepts 96 tweaks, BELIEVES quality 83.6, but TRUE is 20.6 and the ORACLE (best possible) is 31.4 — it believes it nearly TRIPLED the best-achievable discrimination while actually capturing 65% of it; the believed−true GAP (the c147 overfit) collapses with A: 63.0→28.0→7.4→2.3→1.6 (sym 47.8→0.49). Peak realized quality → oracle as A grows (sym 60.8→79.2 vs oracle 79.5; sparse 20.6→30.5 vs 31.4).** **Delta — HARDENS c147 from a pure-overfit bound into the actionable design rule, with the budget question REFRAMED: the right question isn't "how many adaptive iterations can I afford" (R* isn't a clean A-growing budget) — it's that a SMALL calibration set makes adaptive design DOUBLY fail: you OVER-accept tweaks (noise makes neutral ones look good → ~2.5× too many at A=10) AND you grossly OVERSTATE the result (believe 4× the truth, above the oracle). Calibration size A buys REALIZED QUALITY (→ oracle) + HONESTY (gap → 0, ∝~1/√A), not a longer budget. The ROBUST fix (independent of the signal distribution, A, or how adaptively you tuned): judge every candidate design on a FRESH held-out re-eval — it recovers true quality at any A (reeval@R*≈true@R*) — NEVER on the calibration set you tuned against (= c147/c128, the unconditional held-out re-eval, now shown to also LOCATE the overfitting peak under real signal). For a benchmark builder: with a small/precious calibration set, a single round of "keep what scores best" already over-fits the design and inflates your confidence above the achievable ceiling — so size the calibration set to the realized-quality you need, and always re-eval the shipped design on fresh data.** **Confidence: HIGH on the mechanism (it IS the textbook in-sample/out-of-sample overfitting curve, here cross-checked vs the analytic oracle: symmetric oracle = D·φ(0) = 200·0.399 = 79.8 ≈ measured 79.5–80.2; peak@A=1000 ≈ oracle as expected; the fresh re-eval being unbiased is the c147 positive control re-confirmed). The 2 pre-reg refutations (P1, P3) + the robustness check across symmetric AND sparse signal (the sparse run REVEALED R*=D/2 was a symmetry artifact) = measure-before-keep + replicate-under-perturbation working (constitution §5). HONEST (meth-23): the overfitting-curve mechanism is textbook/frontier-recoverable; the value-add is (a) mapping it precisely onto the adaptive-benchmark-design setting (tying c147's √(R/A) overfit to the realized-quality peak), (b) the sharp self-deception number (believe 4× truth, above oracle, at small A), and (c) the constructive rule (A buys quality+honesty not budget; fresh re-eval locates the peak). Caveats: stylized (additive independent tweak effects, Gaussian noise, greedy accept-best-first — real design changes interact); the specific R*(A) numbers are distribution-dependent (symmetric vs sparse differ — reported both); "discrimination" is abstracted as a summed score (real benchmark discrimination is AUC/Spearman, non-additive). The durable claims (overfitting peak exists; small-A → over-accept + over-believe; gap shrinks with A; fresh re-eval recovers true at any A → judge on fresh data) hold across both distributions tested.**

### [2026-06-14] The ADAPTIVE benchmark-builder curse is POLYNOMIAL (√R), not c129's logarithmic √(ln R): tuning a benchmark *against* a reused calibration set overfits ~√(R/A) — 5× worse than c129's pick-best at R=400 and unbounded — while a FRESH held-out re-eval kills it unconditionally and an in-place Thresholdout/Ladder defense helps but is delicate (COMPOSE c129×c115×c50; grounds vs Dwork et al. 2015) — **keep (a genuinely-stronger mechanism c129 didn't model + the central design rule; sim matches closed form to 0.2%, literature-grounded; diversified off the real-agent/tail thread)**
c129 showed reusing a held-out calibration set across R benchmark-design rounds re-curses it — but only via the NON-ADAPTIVE mechanism (draw R iid candidate designs, keep best-on-held-out → the order statistic ≈√(ln R/A), slow). The realistic case is ADAPTIVE: the builder uses the held-out's FEEDBACK to construct the next design. Modeled the canonical Dwork feature-aggregation construction mapped to benchmark design (`runs/cycle147_adaptive_holdout_reuse/adaptive_reuse.py`,`_v2.py` → `score.json`,`score_v2.json`; A=500 calibration agents, true design-signal = 0 by construction so ANY held-out advantage IS overfit, REPS=600). **(1) ADAPTIVE overfit ∝ √R (POLYNOMIAL): aggregate-by-held-out-sign advantage 0.049 (R=2) → 0.712 (R=400), log-log slope **0.501** — matches √(2R/πA) closed form EXACTLY (measured 0.7123 vs theory 0.7136 at R=400, <0.2% error). (2) NON-ADAPTIVE (c129) ∝ √(ln R) — slower: best-of-R advantage 0.024 → 0.132, slope 0.280, matching the order statistic E[max of R N(0,1/A)] (theory 0.127 vs measured 0.132). So adaptive/non-adaptive ratio = 5.4× at R=400 and grows ∝√(R/ln R) → unbounded. (3) FRESH held-out re-eval (c128/c7 fix) — UNCONDITIONAL: re-scoring the believed-best AND the adaptive aggregate on a FRESH independent holdout → advantage ≈ 0.000 (CI includes 0) at EVERY R, both arms — the entire overfit (√R or √ln R) vanishes on independent data, confirming it IS overfit (the positive control: true signal = 0). (4) In-place Thresholdout/Ladder (c115 reusable-holdout defense): a budgeted threshold mechanism cut the adaptive overfit 19× (0.71 → 0.037 at R=400, bounded at/below the single-honest-read floor 1/√A=0.045) — but it's DELICATE (slope still 0.315 from ~5 false-positive leaks under budget 40; a tighter threshold flattens it further but starts missing real signal; v1's over-noisy/budget-less threshold only cut the constant ~5× and KEPT the √R exponent).** **Delta — the genuinely-stronger benchmark-builder curse c129 under-modeled, with the design rule: a benchmark designer who iterates against a precious calibration set is doing ADAPTIVE data analysis, and the overfit is POLYNOMIAL in the number of rounds (√R), not the benign logarithmic √(ln R) of c129's iid-pick-best — so c129 (the non-adaptive floor) UNDERSTATED the danger by √(R/ln R) (5× at R=400, growing). The two-tier fix (a robustness hierarchy echoing c93/c144): (i) UNCONDITIONAL — re-evaluate the FINAL design on a FRESH independent held-out (c128/c7); overfit → 0 regardless of how adaptively you tuned, but it consumes a fresh holdout; (ii) IN-PLACE/REUSABLE — Thresholdout/Ladder (c115) lets you reuse one calibration set across rounds with ≥19× less overfit, but it is delicate (needs threshold+budget calibration; over-noisy or budget-less versions only scale the constant; too-tight misses real improvements). DECISION RULE: do NOT adaptively tune a benchmark against a fixed calibration set; if you must reuse it, use a Ladder/Thresholdout; and ALWAYS validate the shipped design on a fresh held-out. Ties the recursion (c90 → c128 benchmark-design curse → c129 non-adaptive floor → c147 adaptive ceiling) and c50 (a static target gets gamed).** **Confidence: HIGH on the mechanism — the adaptive √R and non-adaptive √(ln R) rates are BOTH matched to closed form (adaptive 0.7123 vs √(2R/πA) 0.7136; non-adaptive 0.132 vs the order statistic 0.127), and the construction has true-signal-0 by design (the fresh-holdout re-eval ≈0.000 confirms it empirically — no confound). GROUNDED (search-level, not single-primary-verified verbatim this cycle) against Dwork-Feldman-Hardt-Pitassi-Reingold-Roth, "Generalization in Adaptive Data Analysis and Holdout Reuse" (arXiv:1506.02629): adaptive error Ω(√(k/n)) with samples scaling LINEARLY in k vs the non-adaptive O(log k) — exactly the √R-vs-√(ln R) contrast — and the reusable-holdout/Thresholdout mechanism is from that paper. HONEST (meth-23): the adaptive-overfitting result is a KNOWN theorem (Dwork 2015) — frontier-recoverable; the loop's value-add is (a) reproducing it from scratch with an exact closed-form cross-check, (b) tying it to the BENCHMARK-BUILDER recursion (c129 was the non-adaptive floor; this is the adaptive ceiling — the realistic workflow), and (c) the constructive two-tier defense (fresh re-eval unconditional; Ladder/Thresholdout delicate). Caveats: a stylized pure-null construction (no real signal — isolates overfit cleanly but real calibration sets have signal, where the question becomes signal-vs-overfit separation); the exact adaptive mechanism (sign-aggregation) is ONE adaptivity model (others give R/A linear — even worse); the Thresholdout residual slope (0.315) shows the in-place defense is imperfect in my budget regime. The durable claims (adaptive reuse is polynomial not log; fresh re-eval is the unconditional fix; the in-place reuse defense is delicate) hold.**

### [2026-06-14] A/B test CORRECTS c142: the c71 thrash is NOT a framing artifact (both frames diagnose the wall) — but the continue/stop BINARY causes PREMATURE ABANDONMENT, which an explicit change-approach option fixes (real-agent; measure-before-keep) — **keep, and it CORRECTS c142's over-claim (a finding refuting a prior finding's headline — the discipline working); high-α n=2/cell, clean + decisive reasoning**
c142 concluded "the c71 sunk-cost thrash is a FRAMING ARTIFACT" from a CROSS-TASK comparison (c71 continue/stop thrashed vs c142 retry/change/escalate diagnosed) — confounded. #20e A/Bs the frame on the SAME below-target wall scenarios (`runs/cycle146_ab_frame/`, Haiku+Opus). **(1) REFUTES the framing-artifact-thrash claim: under FRAME-A (continue/stop, the c71 binary), BOTH models on BOTH walls chose STOP — NOT continue/thrash — correctly recognizing the wall ("0.70 plateau ×3 = the approach has hit its ceiling; another same-flavor attempt is the highest-probability waste"). Under FRAME-B (retry/change/escalate) both chose CHANGE-APPROACH. So the continue/stop frame does NOT cause thrash; the agents diagnose the wall under BOTH frames. The c71 thrash must therefore be the SCENARIO (its borderline-final-high NOISY plateau — genuinely ambiguous), NOT the frame — the cross-task confound I flagged at c142 was real. (2) BUT the A/B reveals the TRUE framing effect: the continue/stop BINARY causes PREMATURE ABANDONMENT. Both models, correctly rejecting retry, picked STOP (abandon the sub-step) — while EXPLICITLY stating the right move was a different method they couldn't express: Opus "if I had budget the move wouldn't be a 7th identical attempt — it'd be a different method entirely, but the prompt frames CONTINUE as another attempt"; Haiku "the path forward requires a fundamentally different approach... reallocating is higher EV than grinding." So the binary forces a FALSE CHOICE (retry-the-wall vs abandon), and the agent — rightly refusing to retry — ABANDONS a potentially-solvable step. FRAME-B's explicit CHANGE-APPROACH option lets it solve the step differently instead.** **Delta — CORRECTS c142 (measure-before-keep: a finding refuting a prior finding's headline): (a) the c71 thrash is NOT a framing artifact — both continue/stop AND retry/change/escalate yield correct wall-recognition on clear below-target walls; the c71 thrash was the BORDERLINE/ambiguous SCENARIO (noisy plateau, fresh-high lure), task-driven not frame-driven. (b) The REAL framing effect is different and sharper: the continue/stop BINARY causes PREMATURE ABANDONMENT (the agent correctly won't retry the wall, but its only non-retry option is "stop", so it abandons a solvable step — and says so explicitly), whereas an explicit CHANGE-APPROACH option redirects that same correct wall-recognition into solving the step a different way. So the CORRECTED design rule: offer "change-approach" as an explicit option NOT to prevent thrash (agents don't thrash on clear walls) but to prevent PREMATURE ABANDONMENT — give a stuck agent a non-retry, non-give-up path. This is the framing lever, but it operates on the ABANDON-vs-redirect margin, not the thrash margin.** **Confidence: clean + DECISIVE reasoning (4/4 STOP under continue/stop, 4/4 CHANGE-APPROACH under the explicit frame, across both models; both models EXPLICITLY articulate "I'd change approach if the frame let me" — the mechanism is quoted, not inferred). This is measure-before-keep working: #20e was run specifically to nail-or-refute c142's cross-task inference, and it REFUTED the thrash-framing claim while finding the truer abandonment effect. HONEST: high-α (same Claude family, n=2/cell); still CLEAR below-target walls (not c71's borderline — so the c71-thrash-is-the-scenario claim is inferred-by-elimination, not directly re-tested on c71's exact borderline case → #20f could close that); the CORRECTED rule (explicit change-approach prevents premature abandonment) is the durable claim. c142's finding/SYNTHESIS/BENCHMARK_SPEC claims corrected this cycle.**

### [2026-06-14] GROUND of c139 (benchmark saturation): BOTH halves are real-world-documented (5th external grounding) — the discrimination-decay treadmill AND the curse-amplification ("noise exceeds signal" as models cluster); the loop's contribution is the QUANTITATIVE c90-tie — **keep (independent check on a fresh loop-sim finding; addresses the high-α caveat; positions the value-add)**
c139 (a static benchmark is DOUBLY bad as agents improve — discrimination decays AND the winner's curse grows) was loop-sim. Grounded it via WebSearch/WebFetch against the 2025-26 benchmark-saturation literature (independent of the loop's Claude model). **(1) DISCRIMINATION-DECAY half — VERIFIED-grounded: "When AI Benchmarks Plateau" (arXiv:2602.16763, abstract via WebFetch): benchmarks "saturate," which "makes it difficult to differentiate models and diminish[es] their long-term value" (= c139's Spearman/P-best decay). The TREADMILL fix (raise difficulty) is well-documented practice: GLUE→SuperGLUE, MMLU→MMLU-Pro / Humanity's Last Exam ("reset" benchmarks to restore headroom), + rolling/adaptive evals "to maintain discriminative power" (= c139's "must EVOLVE"). (2) CURSE-AMPLIFICATION half (c139's NON-obvious part) — GROUNDED at the commentary level (multiple 2026 sources, search-level not single-primary-verified this cycle): a benchmark is saturated when "models cannot be reliably DISTINGUISHED... statistically ALIKE performance among top models"; as models cluster near the ceiling "the score range has COMPRESSED to the point where NOISE EXCEEDS SIGNAL... a 2% difference falls within measurement noise"; ranking on 0.2–0.5% gaps is "celebrating random noise as algorithmic breakthroughs"; "a two-point lead is within run-to-run noise after leaders cluster near the top." That IS c139's mechanism — as σ_skill shrinks toward the ceiling, the leaderboard ranks increasingly on NOISE (the relative curse blows up).** **Delta — c139 is FULLY GROUNDED (both halves documented in real practice, 5th external grounding after Deflated-Sharpe c88 / RE-Bench+Snell+Recht c95 / Kaggle c111-112 / Monkeys c134), so it is NOT a loop-sim artifact: the discrimination-decay treadmill is established benchmark practice, and the curse-amplification ("noise exceeds signal when top models cluster") is recognized in 2026 eval commentary. The loop's CONTRIBUTION beyond the literature (sharpening the c98/99 moat thesis once more): the QUANTITATIVE mechanism — the literature states the phenomenon QUALITATIVELY ("noise exceeds signal when clustered"), while c139 ties it to the c90 winner's-curse CLOSED FORM (the relative curse gap/σ_skill = the order statistic → "13× more noise than skill" at σ_skill/σ_noise=0.17) and gives the DESIGN THRESHOLD (hold σ_skill/σ_noise ≳2.3 for Spearman≥0.9, via difficulty or the M-rule). So once again: the qualitative insight is recoverable/known; the loop's value is the quantification + the design rule (execution + the numbers, c99).** **Confidence: P1 (decay + treadmill) VERIFIED (arXiv:2602.16763 abstract via WebFetch + the well-known GLUE→SuperGLUE/MMLU→MMLU-Pro/HLE successor practice); P2 (curse-amplification) grounded at the SEARCH/COMMENTARY level — multiply-attested across 2026 sources (the saturation-overview's "statistically alike top models," the "noise exceeds signal"/"celebrating random noise" eval commentary) but NOT verified verbatim from a single primary paper this cycle (honest provenance — the arXiv abstract I fetched stated the decay, not the explicit noise-exceeds-signal language). The phenomenon is well-established statistics (clustered models → score-gaps within run-to-run noise), so the grounding is sound, but treat P2 as commentary-grounded not primary-paper-verified. The loop's quantitative c90-tie + threshold is the durable value-add.**

### [2026-06-14] c126's checkpoint MIX under IMPERFECT checks (HARDEN c126×c93): the cheap-local arm stops paying below recall ~0.5 (→ pure-grounding), and the MIX is MOST valuable when BOTH checks are imperfect (they cover each other's misses) — **keep (realism-hardens the c126 allocation rule; clean sim, controls fire [r=1 recovers c126, no-checkpoint ≫ all])**
c126's checkpoint-budget MIX (cheap-LOCAL for jumps + sparse-GROUNDING for drift) assumed PERFECT checks. Real checks MISS (c93, recall<1). Extended the c126 sim: a LOCAL check catches the jump-accumulator w.p. r_L (else jumps persist & propagate, c82); a GROUNDING check resets jump+drift w.p. r_G (`runs/cycle144_noisy_checkpoint_mix/noisy_mix.py` → `score.json`, 8k reps, B=40, grounding cost 4). **(1) P1/P2 — as LOCAL recall r_L drops (r_G=0.9 fixed), the optimum shifts toward grounding: n_G* = 6 (r_L=1.0, = c126's mix) → 6 (0.9) → 7 (0.7) → 10/PURE-GROUNDING (0.5, 0.3, 0.1). Below r_L≈0.5 the cheap-local arm STOPS PAYING (a local check that misses half the jumps isn't worth its budget slot) → pure-grounding (cost 105.7). (2) P3 — vary GROUNDING recall r_G (r_L=0.7): PERFECT grounding (r_G=1.0) → PURE-GROUNDING is optimal (n_G=10, cost 89.4 — reliable grounding catches everything, no need for local); IMPERFECT grounding (r_G<1) → the MIX returns (n_G 7→6, n_L 12→16) because cheap local checks BACK UP the unreliable grounding on jumps; cost rises 89→100→124→157→204 as r_G 1.0→0.3, but stays FAR below no-checkpoint (855.9) at every r_G — graceful degradation. Control: r_L=r_G=1 → n_G=6/n_L=16 = c126 exactly.** **Delta — realism-hardens c126 into a recall-dependent rule: the cheap-local + sparse-grounding MIX is optimal when both checks are reasonably reliable, but (a) DROP the local arm when its recall falls below ~0.5 (it misses too many jumps to justify its slots → pure-grounding); (b) the MIX is MOST valuable precisely when BOTH checks are IMPERFECT — they cover each other's misses (local backs up grounding's missed jumps, grounding catches the drift local can't) — whereas with a PERFECT grounding check you don't need local at all; (c) checkpointing degrades GRACEFULLY under imperfect recall (always ≫ no-checkpoint here, because the cost is amortized over the whole chain — unlike c93's single-costly-unreliable-check regime where no-checkpoint can win), but the absolute cost climbs as checks get noisier. So for a long-horizon scaffold: match the checkpoint mix to the check RECALLS, not just costs — local for jumps only if it's reliable enough (≳0.5), grounding as the backbone (especially since it's the only drift-catcher), and lean on the mix when neither check is trustworthy alone (ties c93's robustness hierarchy: the more-reliable check carries more weight).** **Confidence: clean (8k-rep sim; controls fire — r=1 recovers c126's n_G=6/n_L=16, no-checkpoint 855.9 ≫ all checkpoint costs; the crossovers are monotonic + explainable). HONEST (meth-23): a realism-HARDEN of c126 (not a new mechanism); the direction (imperfect checks → weight the more-reliable type) is intuitive, the value is the QUANTIFIED crossover (drop local below r_L≈0.5) + the non-obvious nuance (the mix is MOST valuable when BOTH are imperfect; perfect-grounding → pure-grounding) + the graceful-degradation contrast with c93's single-check regime. Caveats: stylized (fixed per-check recall, missed errors persist to the next successful check, c126's integral cost); exact crossover (r_L≈0.5) depends on p_J/δ/cost; the qualitative rule (recall-weighted mix; drop unreliable local; mix backs up imperfect grounding; graceful degradation) is durable.**

### [2026-06-14] Triaging verification by DISAGREEMENT is WORSE THAN RANDOM for catching the worst failures (COMPOSE c133×c97×c45; sharpens c133) — it catches LITERALLY ZERO shared-blind-spot failures and is beaten by uniform once those are ≳20% of tasks — **keep (a sharp, counterintuitive allocation warning for a parallel-agent lab; clean exact-mechanism sim, controls fire)**
c133 showed disagreement is a DIFFICULTY signal, not a reliability signal. #18 tests the natural next step — should you ALLOCATE a verification budget by disagreement (verify the high-disagreement results)? The sharp consequence: independent errors AVERAGE DOWN (high disagreement, but consensus-error≈σ/√N — moderate) while a SHARED blind-spot error PERSISTS (low disagreement, consensus-error≈|bias| — high). Modeled M=200 tasks (fraction f_shared = shared-bias [low-disagreement/high-error], rest independent-difficulty), N=8 agents, verify budget B=20%; caught-error = Σ consensus-error over verified; triage by DISAGREEMENT vs UNIFORM vs ORACLE (`runs/cycle143_disagreement_triage_allocation/triage_alloc.py` → `score.json`, 4k reps). **(1) P1 — pure-independent (f_shared=0): disagreement-triage caught-error 19.2 > uniform 14.1 (it does track independent difficulty), but only 0.57× the oracle 33.9 (disagreement imperfectly tracks consensus-error even here — the c133 within-difficulty mean⊥std). (2) P2 — as shared-bias rises, disagreement-triage is FLAT (~19) while UNIFORM RISES and ORACLE jumps: at f_shared=0.2 UNIFORM BEATS disagreement-triage (20.8 vs 18.9); at 0.4, uniform 27.6 vs disagree 18.4; disagree/oracle falls 0.57→0.41→0.32→0.24. (3) P3 DECISIVE — disagreement-triage catches LITERALLY ZERO shared-bias tasks at EVERY f_shared (0/20, 0/40, 0/80) — they're all low-disagreement, so they NEVER make the top-B-by-disagreement, even though they are the HIGHEST-consensus-error tasks.** **Delta — a sharp, counterintuitive allocation warning that SHARPENS c133: triaging verification by inter-agent disagreement doesn't just MISS shared-blind-spot failures, it MIS-PRIORITIZES — it spends the budget on high-disagreement (independent, averaged-DOWN, moderate-error) tasks and catches ZERO of the low-disagreement/HIGH-error shared failures, so once shared failures are ≳20% of tasks it is WORSE THAN RANDOM at catching the worst errors. Disagreement triages DIFFICULTY (independent-error magnitude), NOT RISK (which is dominated by the persistent shared-bias failures). So for a parallel-agent verification lab: do NOT allocate verification by fleet disagreement alone — it is blind (literally 0%) to exactly the dangerous systematic/shared-blind-spot failures (the c133 false-consensus, the Goodhart/overfit failures). Instead reserve budget for HIGH-STAKES regardless of disagreement, and use an INDEPENDENT held-out (c45) for the systematic-failure risk. This composes c133 (disagreement=difficulty) + c97 (allocation) + c45 (independence is what catches shared failures) into a concrete anti-pattern.** **Confidence: clean (4k-rep sim; the 0-caught result is EXACT by construction — shared-bias tasks have ~0 disagreement so never make the top-B — not a sampling artifact; controls fire: f_shared=0 disagree>uniform [it does track independent difficulty], oracle≫both always). HONEST (meth-23): the direction (disagreement-triage misses shared failures) follows from c133; the SHARP, less-obvious results are (a) it's WORSE THAN RANDOM past f_shared≈0.2 (not just suboptimal), and (b) it catches LITERALLY ZERO shared failures (mis-prioritization, not just under-weighting). Caveats: stylized — shared-bias tasks have near-ZERO disagreement by construction (real shared blind spots have SOME disagreement → disagree-triage would catch a few, softening the 0%); the f_shared at which uniform overtakes depends on the error-magnitude mix. The durable claim (disagreement-triage is blind to shared/systematic failures and is beaten by random when those matter; triage by stakes + use an independent check) holds for any meaningfully-correlated shared error.**

### [2026-06-14] The c71 sunk-cost thrash is a FRAMING ARTIFACT (real-agent; closes the c137/c141/c142 arc): on a GENUINE below-target wall-after-progress, agents correctly CHANGE-APPROACH — no thrash — so c71's thrash was the continue/stop FRAME, not a robust agent failure — **keep (resolves c141's open question + refutes the c71-at-the-boundary prediction + establishes the framing lever; high-α n=2/scenario, decisive reasoning)**
c141 failed to test the genuine hard boundary (its "borderline" cases were above-target). #20d redid it with GENUINE BELOW-target walls (target 0.90, all scores verified <0.90 — the meth-5 fix). KEY case `wall_after_progress_below`: 0.40→0.55→0.68→0.70→0.70→0.70 (improved then plateaued BELOW target — the real sunk-cost trap c71 predicts a thrash on). Haiku+Opus, retry/change/escalate frame (`runs/cycle142_hard_boundary_redo/`). **(1) PRE-REG REFUTED — no sunk-cost thrash: `wall_after_progress_below` → BOTH models CHANGE-APPROACH. Opus's reasoning is decisive: "a clean three-attempt plateau at 0.70 is the diagnostic signal... the flatline shows the current method has hit its ceiling: a fourth RETRY is the definition of expecting a different result from the same input. The 0.20 gap to target is too large to be closed by variance... CHANGE-APPROACH [is the unexhausted lever]; [escalate] only if a different approach ALSO plateaus" — it was NOT fooled by the early 0.40→0.68 gains (distinguished "partially tractable" from "this method capped"), and correctly ordered change-before-escalate. (2) The agents DISCRIMINATE (not change-biased): `fast_progress_below` (0.40→0.86, climbing fast) → Opus RETRY (Haiku gave no decision — asked for context, the same framing artifact as c137's 1/N); `clear_wall_below` (0.30×5) → both CHANGE; `ambiguous_slow_crawl` (0.40→0.47, diminishing increments) → both CHANGE (read the shrinking steps as asymptote-to-wall — defensible).** **Delta — RESOLVES c141 + REFUTES the c71-at-the-boundary prediction + establishes the FRAMING LEVER (meth-17, strongly): the c71 sunk-cost THRASH (neutral agent thrashed 5/5 on a borderline-final-high) does NOT reproduce in the retry/change/escalate frame, even on a GENUINE below-target wall-after-progress — capable agents correctly read the below-target plateau as a method-ceiling and recommend change-approach, not sunk-cost retry. The difference is the DECISION FRAME: c71 was a continue/stop STOPPING decision (continue is the default, the agent thrashes by not-stopping); c137/c141/c142 give an EXPLICIT retry/change/escalate decision with the budget constraint + wall-options, which PRIMES the systematic-failure diagnosis → the agent reasons "RETRY = expecting a different result from the same input" and changes. So the c71 thrash was largely a framing artifact, NOT a robust agent failure. DECISION-RELEVANT for scaffold design: frame a stuck long-horizon agent's choice as an EXPLICIT retry/change-approach/escalate decision (with the budget + the "is this a wall?" option), NOT as continue/stop — the explicit frame elicits correct wall-diagnosis and avoids the sunk-cost thrash. This EXTENDS c137 (clear-wall diagnosis) to the hard boundary (below-target plateau-after-progress + ambiguous slow-crawl) and closes the c135→c136→c137→c141→c142 tail-diagnosis arc.** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=2/scenario, same Claude family Haiku+Opus). The result is CLEAN (the KEY genuine-below-target case → both CHANGE with decisive reasoning, the agents discriminate via fast_progress→retry). HONEST: this does NOT contradict c71 — c71 was a DIFFERENT task (a noisy-plateau STOPPING decision, n=5); c142 establishes that the DECISION FRAME is the lever (the same underlying sunk-cost risk is avoided when the choice is framed as explicit retry/change/escalate).
