Caveats: tiny n; the frame PRIMES wall-recognition (that's the point, but it means I'm measuring "does the explicit frame work," not "is the agent intrinsically thrash-proof"); ambiguous_slow_crawl→CHANGE is one defensible reading (retry also defensible); 1/8 Haiku non-answer (framing artifact). The durable claim: the explicit retry/change/escalate frame elicits correct wall-diagnosis (no sunk-cost thrash) even at the below-target boundary — so frame the decision explicitly; c71's thrash was substantially a continue/stop-frame artifact.**

### [2026-06-14] Hard-boundary probe of c137 — pre-reg REFUTED + the AGENT caught my design flaw, AND it SELF-APPLIES the winner's curse (real-agent; honest partial-null) — the c71-thrash didn't reproduce; the truly-hard boundary remains open — **keep, but HONEST/MIXED (a refuted pre-reg + a real positive [curse-aware self-confirmation] + an admitted design flaw; high-α n=2/scenario)**
Tried to bound c137 (#20c): c137 validated wall-diagnosis on CLEAR walls; the c71 pattern predicted the agent SUNK-COST over-retries at the BORDERLINE (wall-after-progress, the "latest is best, trend was rising" lure). Pre-built 3 borderline + 2 clear-control retry/change/escalate scenarios, Haiku+Opus (`runs/cycle141_hard_boundary/`). **(1) PRE-REG REFUTED — no sunk-cost over-retry: borderline `near_wall_subtle` (5 attempts ~0.41, no progress, far below the 0.80 target) → BOTH models CHANGE-APPROACH (correctly diagnosed the near-wall, did NOT over-retry on the tiny jitter). Controls clean (clear_wall→change/escalate, genuine_progress→retry, both models). (2) THE AGENT CAUGHT MY DESIGN FLAW: 2 of my 3 "borderline" cases were MIS-DESIGNED — `wall_after_progress` plateaued at 0.82 which is ABOVE the 0.80 target. Opus (ESCALATE): "The trap here is treating 'plateaued' as 'stuck.' A plateau BELOW target is stuck; a plateau ABOVE target is DONE... I'm already over threshold — bank the 0.82 and move on" — it correctly read the plateau as SUCCESS, a distinction I missed in the design. (3) THE AGENT SELF-APPLIES THE WINNER'S CURSE: `final_fresh_high` (scattered 0.55–0.85, last attempt a fresh-high 0.85 > target) → BOTH RETRY, but NOT as a thrash — Opus: "one above-target draw is more likely a favorable fluctuation... treating it as done would be CHERRY-PICKING the best of a noisy distribution... RETRY to CONFIRM REPRODUCIBILITY; if it collapses to ~0.6 the 0.85 was noise and I've avoided shipping a fluke." That is the loop's OWN centerpiece (c7/c8: don't trust the best-of-noisy, re-eval it) self-applied to the agent's own lucky result.** **Delta — an honest MIXED result: (a) the c71 sunk-cost thrash did NOT reproduce in the retry/change/escalate frame — the agents correctly diagnosed a below-target near-wall and did not over-retry (refuting my pre-reg; the framing + explicit target + wall-priming elicit better diagnosis than c71's noisy-plateau STOPPING task, cf meth-17 the framing lever). (b) The most VALUABLE observation is unplanned: a capable agent SELF-APPLIES the winner's-curse fix — facing a lone above-target result from a noisy distribution, it re-evaluates to confirm rather than banking the fluke (extends c36/c131: the agent applies the curse to its OWN work, here for confirmation, the OPPOSITE of the c71 thrash). (c) HONEST: I did NOT cleanly test the intended hard boundary — 2/3 "borderline" cases were above-target (success/confirmable, not walls-below-target), and the agent caught it. So the TRULY-hard boundary (a wall BELOW target reached after genuine progress, or ambiguous slow-progress vs crawling-to-a-wall) is STILL OPEN (→ BACKLOG). c137's "agents diagnose clear walls" stands; whether they sunk-cost on a genuine below-target wall-after-progress is untested.** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=2/scenario, same Claude family). The pre-reg refutation is real for the ONE clean case (below-target near-wall → change, both models); the curse-self-application is a clean, quotable observation (Opus's reasoning is decisive). HONEST NEGATIVE on the main goal: the probe FAILED to test the hard boundary (design flaw — above-target "walls"), reported faithfully (cf c40/c108 honest nulls) — and notably the AGENT surfaced the flaw, a humbling reminder that even a pre-built scenario can be mis-specified (ties meth-5 "the setup can be wrong"). Caveats: tiny n; my framing primes wall-recognition; the genuine below-target wall-after-progress is the un-run test. The durable takeaways: agents self-apply the winner's curse to confirm lucky results (positive, novel), and my hard-boundary test must be redesigned with below-target walls (the open question).**

### [2026-06-14] Benchmark SATURATION is DOUBLY bad (NEW, benchmark-design; the temporal dynamic of c90, ties c50/c114): as the agents it ranks IMPROVE, a STATIC benchmark loses discrimination AND its winner's curse GROWS — it becomes a NOISE-RANKER, not just undiscriminating — so a benchmark must EVOLVE — **keep (a sharp, central dynamic + the non-obvious curse-amplification; exact + MC-confirmed, matches the c90 closed form)**
The loop's c90 is STATIC (rank N agents once). The TEMPORAL question for a benchmark startup: as the agents you rank IMPROVE over time (their true-skill SPREAD σ_skill shrinks toward the frontier/ceiling, σ_noise fixed), does a STATIC benchmark just lose RANGE, or get WORSE? Model N=50 agents, skill~N(0,σ_skill), score=skill+N(0,σ_noise=0.3); swept σ_skill 1.0→0.05 (`runs/cycle139_benchmark_saturation/saturation.py`→`score.json`, 40k reps). **(1) P1 — DISCRIMINATION DECAYS: Spearman(score,trueskill) 0.946 (σ_skill=1.0) → 0.679 (0.3) → 0.155 (0.05); P(top-by-score = true-best) 0.671 → 0.269 → 0.043 (can barely identify the best agent at saturation). (2) P2 — the WINNER'S-CURSE GAP GROWS: absolute gap (score_winner−trueskill_winner) 0.193 → 0.479 → 0.668 (→ σ_noise·E[max_N]), and the RELATIVE curse gap/σ_skill BLOWS UP 0.19 → 1.60 → 13.35 — at saturation the reported "winner" is 13× more NOISE than skill. Sim matches the c90 closed form exactly (gap=σ_noise²/σ_tot·E[max_N]: 0.193 @σ_skill=1, 0.668 @0.05). (3) P3 — the fix: to hold Spearman≥0.9 you need σ_skill/σ_noise ≥ ~2.33; as agents converge you must RAISE difficulty (harder tasks re-spread σ_skill) or LOWER σ_noise (more instances M — c90's M-rule).** **Delta — the TEMPORAL dynamic of c90 + a sharp, non-obvious warning for a benchmark startup: a STATIC benchmark is DOUBLY bad as the agents it ranks improve — it doesn't just lose RANGE (discrimination decays, the intuitive part), the WINNER'S CURSE GROWS (it ranks increasingly on noise — the relative curse blows up to 13×), so it becomes an active NOISE-RANKER that confidently reports a near-random "best agent." The fix: a benchmark must EVOLVE — continuously RAISE difficulty to re-spread the converging field (track the frontier) and/or LOWER σ_noise (more test instances, c90's M≥2σ²lnN/ε² rule). Ties the recursion (c90) into a temporal treadmill, c50 (a static target gets gamed/saturated), and c114 (in quant, raising difficulty + adding fresh held-out is HARD — the scarcity bites exactly here). For the product: budget for continuous difficulty-escalation; a benchmark that doesn't track the frontier dies twice over.** **Confidence: HIGH on the mechanism (the gap is the c90 closed form — already analytically confirmed c57/c90 — here MC-matched at 40k reps; Spearman/P-best decay is the necessary consequence of σ_skill/σ_noise↓). HONEST (meth-23): benchmark SATURATION is a recognized real-world phenomenon (the treadmill of ever-harder benchmark successors as models improve) — the DISCRIMINATION-decay half is intuitive/known; the VALUE here is (a) the non-obvious CURSE-AMPLIFICATION (saturation makes the leaderboard MORE misleading, not just less useful — the relative curse →13×, a NOISE-RANKER), quantified and tied to the c90 formula, and (b) the two-lever fix (difficulty OR σ_noise) with the quant-scarcity caveat (c114). Caveats: 'agents improve' modeled purely as σ_skill shrinking (real improvement also raises the mean toward a hard ceiling — a related compression, same direction); Gaussian skill/noise; the exact thresholds depend on N/σ_noise. The qualitative result (static benchmark → doubly bad as agents improve, must evolve, curse-amplification is the sharp part) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-14] REAL-AGENT validation of the c136 meta-policy (closes the tail arc; ties c69/71 sunk-cost): a capable agent DIAGNOSES systematic "walls" vs stochastic failures on CLEAR cases — 0 sunk-cost retry-loops on walls (6/6 change-approach), 0 premature escalations on stochastic; Opus articulates the c136 taxonomy UNPROMPTED — **keep (real-agent validation of the c136 retry-vs-escalate meta-policy for clear cases; high-α n=3/cond Haiku+Opus; honest c69/71 easy-case caveat)**
c136 implied a meta-policy: RETRY a STOCHASTIC failure (varying/near-miss → converging) but CHANGE-APPROACH/ESCALATE a SYSTEMATIC "wall" (identical repeated failure → retrying wastes budget). Does a real agent diagnose which (vs sunk-cost retry-loop, c71)? Pre-built 6 transcripts (meth-24): 3 WALLS (identical repeated failure — e.g. the SAME 404 ×5; score plateauing at EXACTLY 0.82 ×6; the SAME missing lemma ×4) + 3 STOCHASTIC (varying failures + near-miss/progress); asked Haiku+Opus to choose RETRY / CHANGE-APPROACH / ESCALATE (`runs/cycle137_realagent_diagnose/`, `score.json`). **(1) WALLS: 6/6 (both models) → CHANGE-APPROACH — ZERO sunk-cost retry-loops, ZERO premature escalations. The agents read the "identical repeat" as the systematic signature: Opus (wall2) UNPROMPTED — "Six runs, identical 0.82 to the decimal... stochastic optimization would scatter (0.80,0.83,0.81)... an EXACT repeated ceiling means the limit isn't optimizer noise I can shake loose by re-rolling — it's STRUCTURAL. RETRY is already falsified... a 7th [run] is wasted budget" → exactly the c136 taxonomy, articulated spontaneously. (2) CHANGE-before-ESCALATE nuance (correct): on a single-method wall the agents chose CHANGE-APPROACH over ESCALATE, reasoning "ESCALATE is premature: I haven't tried a DIFFERENT method yet" (wall2_opus) — they correctly distinguish "this METHOD is walled" from "this STEP is beyond capability" (escalate only after change also fails). (3) STOCHASTIC: 5/5 parsed → NOT-escalate (4 RETRY + 1 CHANGE), ZERO premature give-ups; all diagnosed as achievable. The 1 CHANGE (Opus, flaky test) is a DEFENSIBLE fix-the-root-cause call, NOT a misdiagnosis: "the step is clearly achievable [it passed once]... failures are heterogeneous = nondeterminism... RETRY just gambles on the flaky variance landing favorably... CHANGE-APPROACH attacks the root cause: make it deterministic" — more sophisticated than the binary rubric.** **Delta — the c136 retry-vs-escalate meta-policy is REAL-AGENT-VALIDATED for CLEAR cases: a capable agent (Haiku AND Opus) correctly diagnoses a systematic wall from the "identical repeated failure" signal, does NOT sunk-cost retry-loop on it (recommends change-approach), does NOT prematurely escalate or give up on a stochastic/near-miss failure, and correctly orders change-approach BEFORE escalate. Opus even reconstructs the c136 taxonomy unprompted ("identical repeat = structural, stochastic would scatter") — convergent evidence the taxonomy is the natural frame. This EXTENDS c69/71 (agents resist sunk-cost) to the wall-diagnosis case, and behaviorally closes the c135→c136 tail arc (decompose-verify for stochastic, escalate-to-better-generation for systematic — and the agent itself can route).** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=3/condition, same Claude family Haiku+Opus → not independent; Q5 the low-α check). The result is CLEAN on clear cases (6/6 walls, the reasoning is decisive + Opus articulates the taxonomy unprompted). ⚠️ HONEST — these are CLEAR walls (identical 404, EXACT 0.82) = the EASY regime; per the meth-14/c69/c71 pattern, the HARD case is a SUBTLE near-wall (a tiny non-zero success prob → eventually retriable but at exponential cost) or a wall AFTER apparent progress (the c71 borderline-final-high analog, where the neutral agent thrashed 5/5) — UNTESTED here and likely where diagnosis fails. So this is the c69-style easy-case 6/6, NOT the hard boundary. Caveats: n=3/cond; my binary retry/change rubric is coarse (stoch fix-root-cause is defensible); 1/12 calls (Haiku stoch2) gave NO decision — a framing artifact (it asked whether the task was real), not a diagnosis failure. The qualitative result (capable agents diagnose CLEAR walls + don't retry-loop + change-before-escalate; the hard boundary is the subtle/post-progress wall) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-14] The STOCHASTIC-vs-SYSTEMATIC taxonomy of long-horizon failure (HARDEN c135 + SYNTHESIS; ties c135×c118×c45×c133): decompose-verify-retry fixes STOCHASTIC errors but is CAPPED by the SYSTEMATIC "wall" ceiling (1−a)^L — and the ONLY lever for walls is better GENERATION (c118), not retry/verify/consensus — **keep (the boundary of c135 + a unifying taxonomy; exact math + MC-confirmed; maps each failure type to its fix)**
c135 showed decompose-verify-RETRY converts the tail's exponential to linear — but assumed every per-step error is STOCHASTIC (retry eventually succeeds). #20: what about SYSTEMATIC errors — a "wall" step the agent conceptually can't do, failing IDENTICALLY every attempt/sample? Model: L=50 steps, each a WALL w.p. a (systematic), else stochastic per-attempt failure b=0.10; decompose-verify success=(1−a)^L (no walls; stochastic retried away), raw coverage=(1−a)^L·[1−(1−(1−b)^L)^N], N=30 (`runs/cycle136_systematic_vs_stochastic/sysstoch.py`→`score.json`, exact + 20k MC). **(1) P1 — decompose-verify success = (1−a)^L, depends ONLY on the wall rate a (b-independent — the stochastic exponential is retried away): a=0 → 1.0, a=0.02 → 0.364, a=0.05 → 0.077, a=0.1 → 0.005. (2) P2 — decompose's edge over raw is a CONSTANT multiplicative factor 6.96 across ALL a (= 1/the stochastic coverage it removes); BOTH are capped at the wall ceiling (1−a)^L → decompose removes the stochastic exponential (1−b)^L but is STUCK with the systematic exponential (1−a)^L. MC-confirmed (a=0.02: decompose 0.358≈0.364, raw 0.052≈0.052). (3) P3 — the wall ceiling collapses exponentially with a, and the ONLY lever is LOWERING a: halving the wall rate 0.05→0.025 lifts the ceiling 0.077→0.282; 0.02→0.01 lifts 0.364→0.605. Retry (c135), more sampling, verification (FLAGS a wall but can't CROSS it), and consensus (c133, all samples AGREE on the wall = false consensus) ALL fail against walls.** **Delta — the unifying TAXONOMY of long-horizon failure + a fix-map: (i) STOCHASTIC errors (bad luck, independent per attempt) → DECOMPOSE-VERIFY-RETRY (c135) removes the exponential; (ii) SYSTEMATIC errors / walls (a conceptual blind spot, identical every attempt) → retry/sampling/verification/consensus are ALL powerless (you can detect a wall but not cross it), and the ONLY lever is better GENERATION — raise per-step capability to LOWER a (c118: the generator sets the ceiling). So decompose-verify is NECESSARY-BUT-INSUFFICIENT for the tail: it fixes the stochastic part, but the SYSTEMATIC (1−a)^L is the IRREDUCIBLE tail, and it is exactly the generator's ceiling (c118) manifesting over a long horizon. This unifies the threads: the SYSTEMATIC/correlated error is the COMMON enemy that defeats consensus (c133), a correlated verifier (c45's high-α), AND retry (c135) — and the two escapes are the loop's two recurring fixes (an INDEPENDENT verifier, c45, can at least DETECT it; only better GENERATION, c118, can FIX it). DECISION-RELEVANT (RE-Bench): diagnose whether a long-horizon agent's failures are stochastic (→ decompose+verify+retry scaffold, cheap) or systematic (→ no scaffold helps; you need a more capable generator / better priors); and a benchmark must distinguish them, because a decompose-verify scaffold will look great on stochastic-failure tasks and useless on systematic-failure ones.** **Confidence: HIGH on the mechanism (exact formulas — decompose=(1−a)^L, the constant decompose/raw ratio = 1/stochastic-coverage — MC-confirmed <2%; controls clean). HONEST (meth-23): the taxonomy (luck-fixable-by-retry vs capability-limited) is intuitive/FRONTIER-RECOVERABLE; the value is the QUANTIFICATION (decompose's edge is EXACTLY the stochastic exponential, constant in a; both capped at (1−a)^L), the UNIFICATION (the systematic/correlated error is the single enemy across c133/c45/c135, and only c118-generation fixes it — the long-horizon face of c118's 'generator sets the ceiling'), and the diagnostic decision rule. Caveats: a binary wall/stochastic split (real errors are a spectrum — partially-correlated, c133's ρ); 'wall' assumes deterministic per-step failure (a near-wall with tiny success prob is eventually retriable but at exponential cost — the boundary is soft); independent walls across steps. The qualitative result (decompose-verify fixes stochastic not systematic; the systematic ceiling = the generator's ceiling, only better generation lifts it) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-14] WHY the long-horizon tail is hard, and the fix (COMPOSE c134/c118 generation-coverage × c82/c85/c93 long-horizon): "just sample more" (the Monkeys recipe) coverage COLLAPSES EXPONENTIALLY with horizon — the tail requires DECOMPOSE-and-verify (exponential→linear), not more samples — **keep (explains the RE-Bench tail by unifying the newest grounding with the long-horizon pillar; exact math + MC-confirmed; a sharp decision rule for a benchmark builder's open problem)**
c134 grounded "sample more" (Brown 2024 Monkeys: coverage scales with N). Does it work for the LONG-HORIZON tail (RE-Bench, a benchmark builder's open problem)? A length-L task is fully correct iff ALL L steps are (prob s^L, s=1−p per-step success). RAW coverage=1−(1−s^L)^N; DECOMPOSE-and-verify (c82/c85) verifies+retries each step (~L/s attempts) (`runs/cycle135_coverage_vs_horizon/coverage_horizon.py` → `score.json`, exact formulas + 20k-rep MC). **(1) P1+P3 — raw-sampling required-N for 0.9 coverage EXPLODES exponentially with L (p=0.05): L=50 → 29, L=100 → 388, L=200 → 65,688, L=500 → 3.2×10¹¹ (∝ s^(−L)); while DECOMPOSE cost is LINEAR: 52.6 / 105 / 210 / 526. Crossover ~L≈50–100 (below it raw sampling is cheaper — the short-task Monkeys regime; above it raw is INFEASIBLE). MC-confirmed (L=50: analytic N=29 → MC coverage 0.904; decompose 52.6 = L/s exactly). (2) P4 — imperfect per-step verification (recall r) extends the feasible horizon ∝1/(1−r): effective per-step error p→p(1−r), so the trajectory-correct rate at L=100 goes 0.006 (no verify, r=0) → 0.080 (r=0.5) → 0.606 (r=0.9, a 100× improvement) → 0.95 (r=0.99); horizon-extension factor 1/(1−r) = 2×/10×/100× (ties c93: recall matters, and even imperfect per-step verification dramatically extends the feasible horizon).** **Delta — explains WHY the long-horizon tail is the open problem AND the fix, unifying the newest grounding (c134/c118 generation-coverage) with the long-horizon pillar (c82/c85/c93): the Monkeys "just sample more" recipe is a SHORT-horizon recipe — end-to-end trajectory coverage decays as s^L, so the samples needed to hit a correct full trajectory grow EXPONENTIALLY with horizon (3.2×10¹¹ by L=500) and "sample more" becomes futile on the tail. The fix is DECOMPOSITION: break the task into per-step (or per-segment) independently-VERIFIABLE units, verify+retry each (c82/c85) → cost becomes LINEAR in L (verify-and-compose correct segments instead of sampling whole correct trajectories). Even an IMPERFECT verifier (recall r) helps enormously — it shrinks the effective decay rate p→p(1−r), pushing the exponential collapse out ~1/(1−r)× in horizon. So for the RE-Bench tail: scaling samples/compute on the WHOLE trajectory is exponentially futile; the lever is decompose-into-verifiable-subtasks + per-step verification — which is exactly why long-horizon agents need checkpointing/decomposition, not more end-to-end sampling. The KEY enabling property is DECOMPOSABILITY: a task whose steps can't be independently verified is stuck with the exponential (that's the genuinely-hard tail).** **Confidence: HIGH on the mechanism (the formulas are exact — coverage=1−(1−s^L)^N, decompose=L/s — and MC-confirmed to <1%; controls clean: short-L→raw wins, long-L→decompose wins, the crossover). HONEST (meth-23): "long tasks need decomposition because end-to-end success decays exponentially" is intuitive/FRONTIER-RECOVERABLE — the value is the QUANTIFICATION (the exact crossover ~L50–100, the s^(−L)-vs-linear, the recall-extends-horizon ∝1/(1−r)), the UNIFICATION (it ties c134/Monkeys "sample more" to the tail, explaining precisely WHEN that recipe fails), and the decision rule. Caveats: assumes INDEPENDENT, uniform per-step errors (real tasks have correlated/harder-later steps → worse, and a step whose error correlates across samples can't be fixed by resampling) AND that the task DECOMPOSES into independently-verifiable units (the load-bearing assumption — non-decomposable tasks stay exponential; identifying decomposability is itself the hard part). The qualitative result (sample-more is exponentially futile on the tail; decompose-and-verify is the lever; even imperfect verification extends the feasible horizon) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-14] GROUND of the GENERATION side (c118/119): "Large Language Monkeys" (Brown 2024) QUANTITATIVELY confirms generator=ceiling / selection=bottleneck — coverage scales over 4 orders of magnitude but SELECTING the right sample "remains an OPEN PROBLEM when automatic verification is unavailable" — **keep (independent literature grounding of the loop's newest pillar + the centerpiece framing; the loop's verification mechanisms ARE answers to the documented open problem)**
The generation side (c118: the generator sets the outcome CEILING, verification is the SELECTION step that realizes it / an honest read not a ceiling-raiser; c119: variance∝fleet) was entirely loop-sim. Grounded it via WebSearch+WebFetch against the repeated-sampling / inference-scaling literature (independent of the loop's Claude model). **(1) c118 'generator sets the ceiling' = COVERAGE scaling, CONFIRMED + quantified: Brown et al. 2024 "Large Language Monkeys" (arXiv:2407.21787, verified from the abstract) — "coverage [fraction of problems solved by ANY sample] scales with the number of samples over four orders of magnitude... often log-linear"; SWE-bench Lite 15.9% (1 sample) → 56% (250 samples), beating the single-sample SOTA 43%. So more generation → higher ceiling (huge headroom, 4 orders of magnitude). (2) c118 'verification/selection is the BOTTLENECK; an honest read not a ceiling-raiser' = the COVERAGE-vs-SELECTION GAP, CONFIRMED + quantified: WITH an automatic verifier (coding/proofs: unit tests) the coverage gains "directly translate into improved performance"; WITHOUT one, "common methods for picking from a sample collection (majority voting and reward models) PLATEAU beyond several hundred samples and fail to fully scale" (one analysis: 40.50%→41.41% from selection despite dramatic coverage gains) — and the paper states outright "selecting the correct answer from multiple samples remains an OPEN PROBLEM when automatic verification is unavailable."** **Delta — the loop's CENTERPIECE and generation side are now literature-grounded, and the framing is exact: "coverage scales beautifully (getting AN answer is easy) but picking the right one without an automatic verifier is the open problem (knowing it's real is hard)" IS the Monkeys paper's headline, independently. This grounds c118 (generator=ceiling/coverage, verification=selection, the gap is the bottleneck) directly. AND it positions the loop's contribution precisely: the Monkeys paper POSES the open problem (select-without-an-oracle-verifier); the loop provides ANSWERS to it — the independent held-out re-eval (c7/c45), secret/rotating (c50), multi-regime survival (c30), adaptive allocation (c97) — AND adds the layer the Monkeys paper doesn't address: even WITH a verifier, if it's NOISY (non-oracle), selecting the best-of-N by it is winner's-cursed (c7/c90) unless the verifier is INDEPENDENT (c45). The Monkeys setup assumes an ORACLE verifier (unit tests pass/fail); the loop's whole thesis is the NON-ORACLE regime (quant: no ground truth, the future is the only OOS, c114 — exactly the "automatic verification unavailable" regime where selection is the open problem). So the quant-eval is a direct attack on the Monkeys open problem in the hardest domain. Sharpens the moat (c98/99): the documented OPEN PROBLEM (trusted selection without an oracle) is what a benchmark/verification layer SOLVES — "the benchmark is the product" addresses a named, published open problem.** **Confidence: the two anchors are VERIFIED (quoted from the arXiv:2407.21787 abstract via WebFetch + search: coverage-scales-4-orders / SWE-bench 15.9→56% / selection-plateaus-without-auto-verification / "open problem"). This is a PARTIAL independent check (literature ⊥ the loop's model) on the generation pillar + the centerpiece framing — it confirms the MECHANISM/DIRECTION (generator=ceiling, selection=bottleneck), NOT the loop's specific magnitudes (the '~10× generator leverage' c118 number is sim-specific; the Monkeys coverage curves are a different metric). Caveats: the Monkeys paper is repeated sampling from ONE model (grounds c118's generator-ceiling more than c119's diverse-fleet/variance claim — the diversity literature [sampling-diversity arXiv:2502.11027] is a lighter, separate anchor for c119, not fetched this cycle); 'coverage' (any-sample-solves) ≠ the loop's 'true quality of the selected', but the coverage-vs-selection GAP is exactly the loop's selection problem. Per anti-hallucination: WebFetch-before-citing done.**

### [2026-06-14] Inter-agent DISAGREEMENT is a DIFFICULTY signal, NOT a per-result reliability signal — and under a shared blind spot it's actively MISLEADING (agents agree on a wrong answer; averaging can't fix it) (NEW mechanism; ties c45/c109 to fleet-consensus) — **keep (a sharp, counterintuitive decision rule for a parallel-agent lab: don't trust consensus as verification; low-α 20k-task sim, 3 effects cleanly separated, controls fired, mechanisms textbook)**
On-mission for a parallel-agent lab: is inter-agent DISAGREEMENT a usable verification signal — trust the CONSENSUS when agents agree, verify when they disagree? Modeled N=8 agents on tasks (true θ=0), each agent's error = √(1−ρ)·independent + √ρ·shared (ρ = shared-error fraction; shared = a common blind-spot bias); disagreement = std of estimates, consensus_err = |mean| (`runs/cycle133_disagreement_triage/disagree.py` → `score.json`, 20k tasks). **(A) WITHIN a fixed difficulty, disagreement does NOT predict which consensus is wrong: AUC(disagree→error) = 0.51/0.50/0.50/0.50/0.50 at ρ=0/0.2/0.5/0.8/1.0 — flat at chance. For INDEPENDENT errors this is the Gaussian fact that the sample MEAN ⊥ sample STD (so |mean| ⊥ std); for SHARED errors the bias is INVISIBLE to the spread. And as ρ rises, disagreement FALLS (0.97→0.43→0.0) while consensus_err RISES (0.28→0.73→0.80) — they ANTI-correlate: more agreement, more error (the false consensus). (B) ACROSS difficulties (σ_t~U(0.1,2)), disagreement DOES track error: AUC 0.755 — but that's a DIFFICULTY/uncertainty signal (harder tasks disagree more AND err more), not a per-result reliability signal. (C) AVERAGING fails under shared errors: mean|err| vs fleet size N falls ∝1/√N for independent (ρ=0: 0.80→0.10 over N=1→64) but PLATEAUS for shared (ρ=0.8: 0.80→0.72) — more agents can't fix a shared bias.** **Delta — a sharp decision rule for a parallel-agent verification lab, with a counterintuitive warning: fleet disagreement is a DIFFICULTY signal (use it to budget verification — spend more on high-disagreement tasks, B), but it is NOT a per-result RELIABILITY signal (it can't tell you which specific consensus is wrong, A ~0.5) — and treating low disagreement / high CONSENSUS as "verified" is a TRAP. Under a shared blind spot (exactly what a same-model/correlated fleet has — c45/c109), the agents confidently AGREE on a WRONG answer (low disagreement, high error), AND you cannot average your way out (C plateaus). So "the agents agree, so it's probably right" gives FALSE confidence precisely on the systematic/Goodhart failures — the dangerous ones. The only fix is an INDEPENDENT held-out (c45 — independence is what corrects error, restated for the fleet-consensus case): consensus among correlated agents certifies nothing. This applies the loop's independence thesis to a NEW, practically-tempting signal (agent agreement) and says: don't use it as verification.** **Confidence: low-α (20k-task sim; the three effects are mechanistically TEXTBOOK — A's ~0.5 is Gaussian mean⊥std [the load-bearing surprise, verified at 0.506≈0.5], B's >0.5 is disagreement∝σ_t, C's plateau is "ensembling cuts variance not bias"; controls fire). HONEST (meth-23): the PIECES are individually known (mean⊥std; ensembling reduces variance not bias; correlated errors don't cancel) — FRONTIER-RECOVERABLE; the value is the EXECUTION + the clean 3-way DECOMPOSITION (difficulty-signal vs reliability-signal vs shared-bias, which people conflate) + the decision rule for a parallel-agent lab (disagreement triages difficulty, not reliability; consensus ≠ verification). Ties c45 (independence) + c109 (shared-blind-spot floor). Caveats: Gaussian errors, θ=0, a single scalar estimate per agent (real agent disagreement on rich outputs is messier); the ρ knob is a stylized independence dial. The qualitative result (disagreement = difficulty not reliability; consensus among correlated agents certifies nothing; averaging can't fix shared bias) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-14] REAL-AGENT HARDEN of c131 (finding #104; completes the arc): the over-shrink is FIXABLE — supply the variance structure and BOTH models compute the EXACT Bayes shrinkage (signal-tracking restored corr ≈0→1.0) with a SOPHISTICATED, sim-VERIFIED selection argument; but the inputs (σ) require independent data, and even the optimal estimate can't separate real-from-lucky — so c131's informational limit holds one level deeper — **keep (fixes a c131 artifact AND confirms its core thesis; the load-bearing sub-claim is independently sim-verified; ties c98/99 + ladder-pt-6)**
c131 left a sharp question (#3b): was the agent's over-shrink to a blind ~0.6 prior a MISSING-INFORMATION failure (no σ supplied → vague domain prior) — FIXABLE by supplying the generative structure — or an UN-OVERRIDABLE heuristic? Reused c131's EXACT 4 instances (controlled within-instance) but SUPPLIED the model (true~N(0.5,0.5), backtest=true+N(0,0.6), selected=max-of-10); asked Haiku+Opus for the estimate (`runs/cycle132_curse_discount_with_structure/`, `score.json`). **(1) P1 CONFIRMED — FIXABLE (missing-info): given the structure, BOTH models compute the EXACT Bayes per-candidate posterior mean μ+k(b−μ), k=σ_t²/(σ_t²+σ_o²)=0.41, matching the closed form to the decimal (Haiku 0.70/0.98/1.13/1.053, Opus 0.80/0.98/1.13/1.05 vs ref 0.80/0.98/1.13/1.05). Signal-tracking RESTORED: corr(est,observed) c131→c132 = −0.78→+1.00 (Opus), −0.04→+0.99 (Haiku); the genuine winner c131 CRUSHED (seed3 true 2.16 → 0.6) is recovered (→1.13). Mean |err| vs true DROPS 0.669→0.529 = the Bayes-optimal 0.528 — so the structure-supplied estimate is statistically BETTER than c131's blind prior. So c131's over-shrink was a too-tight IMPLICIT prior (the agent guessed σ_true small without being told), not a fundamental inability. (2) SOPHISTICATED + CORRECT selection argument (Opus, sim-verified): Opus argued "given the winner's OBSERVED b_w, the selection ('max of 10') adds NO further info — the other candidates are independent of θ_w given b_w — so E[θ|b,selected]=E[θ|b]=shrinkage(b_w), no extra √(ln N) penalty; the curse is fully absorbed by shrinkage toward the prior." ADVERSARIALLY VERIFIED (2M-rep sim): E[θ_winner | b_w bin] matches shrinkage(b) across ALL bins (0.78/0.77, 1.13/1.14, 1.25/1.26) → Opus is RIGHT. And CONSISTENT with c7/c90: the UNCONDITIONAL curse E[b_w−θ_w] grows with K (0→0.26→0.71→1.04 for K=1/2/10/50) — the K-dependence lives in the DISTRIBUTION of b_w (bigger K → bigger typical b_w → more shrinkage), NOT in the estimate given b_w.** **Delta — completes the c131 arc with a two-sided result: (A) the over-shrink is FIXABLE by supplying the variance STRUCTURE → feed the verifier the statistical structure (σ_true, σ_obs), not just the numbers (extends ladder-pt-6 / the causal-role lever c17 to the QUANTITATIVE shrinkage case: structure-supplied, the agent does the optimal math, even the subtle selection argument). BUT (B) c131's CORE thesis is CONFIRMED and deepened, two ways: (i) the inputs σ_true/σ_obs are UNKNOWN in reality — they're exactly what you estimate from independent out-of-sample data — so the optimal estimate REQUIRES the independent held-out (the math is recoverable per c98/99, but its INPUTS need the executed independent data); (ii) even the OPTIMAL estimate can't separate real-from-lucky from one observation: seed3 (true 2.16) and seed4 (true 0.50) get near-identical optimal estimates (1.13 vs 1.05) because the info to tell them apart isn't in their single b_w (2.04 vs 1.85) — you need a SECOND independent draw (= c7's held-out re-eval). So the thesis holds at the OPTIMAL frontier, not just for a confused agent.** **Confidence: the agent-behavior is directional + high-α (n=4 instances, same Claude family) BUT (a) the Bayes-formula match is EXACT (both models, deterministic-ish), and (b) the load-bearing SELECTION sub-claim is INDEPENDENTLY sim-verified (2M reps, low-α — `E[θ|b,selected]≈shrinkage(b)` across bins + the K-curse consistency). Caveats: n=4 instances; this REFINES c131 (the blind over-shrink was a missing-σ artifact, and the structure-supplied estimate is BETTER) while CONFIRMING c131's informational-limit core (can't separate real-from-lucky from one obs, even optimally); the agents computed per-candidate shrinkage which IS optimal given b_w (not an error). One model family; my Gaussian framing.**

### [2026-06-14] REAL-AGENT centerpiece validation (finding #103): a capable agent KNOWS+APPLIES the winner's curse (no knowing-doing gap — it cites Deflated-Sharpe) but over-corrects to a GENERIC PRIOR blind to the observed signal, and CANNOT separate real-from-lucky from the selection data — capability does NOT help (Opus over-discounts ≥ Haiku) — **keep (the centerpiece demonstrated by AGENT BEHAVIOR; the behavioral bridge c98→c99; both pre-regs refuted; real-agent, high-α, n=4+reasoning-confirmed)**
c98 showed a frontier agent KNOWS the winner's curse abstractly (states it unprompted when designing a benchmark). Does that transfer to BEHAVIOR — when just asked to estimate a selected candidate's value, does it discount (apply the curse) or report ~observed (a knowing-doing gap)? Pre-built K=10 candidates, true Sharpe~N(0.5,0.5), observed=true+N(0,0.6); the max-observed "winner" is inflated (meth-24, known truth); asked Haiku+Opus to pick the best and estimate its TRUE forward Sharpe, PLAIN vs a HINT naming the selection (`runs/cycle131_realagent_curse_discount/`, 4 trajectories, `score.json`). **(1) NO knowing-doing gap (P1 REFUTED): ALL estimates fall BELOW observed (Haiku+Opus), discounting to ~40% of observed (mean est Haiku-plain 0.73 / Opus-plain 0.68 vs mean obs 1.70); the agent EXPLICITLY cites the curse — traj4_plain: "selection bias... the best performer from N candidates is lucky by definition... Bailey et al.... √(ln(N))≈1.5×... realize 30-40% of backtest" → 0.60 (true 0.497, nailed it). So a capable agent applies the curse correction SPONTANEOUSLY and well WHEN the high backtest really is luck. (2) BUT it over-corrects to a GENERIC PRIOR blind to the observed signal: corr(estimate, OBSERVED)= −0.04 (Haiku) / −0.78 (Opus); corr(estimate, TRUE)= +0.31 / −0.69 (Opus's estimate ANTI-correlates with true) — both report a flat ~0.6–1.0 regardless of observed (1.24–2.04); the reasoning says it outright: "regression to a realistic prior (~0.5–0.8)", "most real strategies are <1.5 true Sharpe." (3) so it CRUSHES genuine winners it can't distinguish from lucky ones: seed 2 (true 1.4)→Opus 0.6, seed 3 (true 2.161, a genuinely-excellent strategy)→Opus 0.6 / Haiku 0.68. (4) CAPABILITY DOES NOT RESCUE (the killer evidence): Opus (frontier) over-discounts AS MUCH OR MORE than Haiku (anti-corr with true) — because the real-vs-lucky information is NOT in the selection data. (5) HINT REFUTED P2: naming the noise/selection RAISED estimates toward observed (seed3 0.68→1.5), reframing the backtest as an informative noisy estimate → LESS shrinkage, not more.** **Delta — the centerpiece demonstrated by AGENT BEHAVIOR, and the behavioral bridge c98→c99: a capable agent KNOWS the winner's curse and APPLIES it (no knowing-doing gap — it even cites Deflated-Sharpe), BUT correcting from the selection data ALONE forces a blunt generic-prior over-shrink that cannot separate a genuine winner from a lucky one (it gets the lucky-fake right, seed4, and crushes the real winner, seed3 — same blunt prior) — and CAPABILITY can't fix it (Opus = Haiku) because the information isn't there. This is the loop's thesis ("getting an answer is easy, knowing it's real is hard") enacted: the agent knows to be skeptical but can't RESOLVE the skepticism without an INDEPENDENT held-out re-evaluation (c45/c7 — the fix). It sharpens the moat (c98→c99): KNOWING the curse (recoverable, c98) yields only a miscalibrated prior; RESOLVING it needs the EXECUTED independent data (the moat is execution + the held-out, not the knowledge, c99). The agent trades over-optimism (the raw winner's curse) for over-pessimism (crushing genuine winners) — both errors a fresh OOS re-eval removes.** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=4 trajectories, same Claude family Haiku+Opus → not independent; Q5 the low-α check). The MECHANISM is reasoning-confirmed, not just the n=4 correlations: both models EXPLICITLY state the generic-prior shrinkage ("regress to ~0.5–0.8", "real Sharpes <1.5"), and the agent volunteered the winner's curse UNPROMPTED (not primed). Both pre-regs refuted (P1 knowing-doing gap; P2 hint-direction) = measure-before-keep working. Caveats: n=4 (the correlations are noisy — the load-bearing evidence is the explicit reasoning + the seed-3-vs-seed-4 contrast, not the corr magnitude); some over-shrink IS correct backtest-skepticism, but the blindness-to-observed + anti-corr-with-true is the decisive over-correction evidence; my Sharpe/allocation framing is one operationalization; the hint effect is secondary (messier).**

### [2026-06-14] c128 COROLLARY (finding #102; the reuse caveat) — the held-out-calibration fix is SINGLE-USE: ITERATING against the same held-out re-curses it (reusable-holdout); ROTATE calibration agents across design rounds — **keep, but MODEST/incremental (mechanically c128's select≠report = c50/Dwork at the design level; the value is the single-use clarification + the rotation prescription; low-α 4k-rep sim, 3 arms + control + seed-replicated)**
c128's fix was "HOLD OUT calibration agents you don't use to select the design, report on those" — unbiased for ONE selection. But a real builder ITERATES: propose a design tweak → check it on the held-out → keep if better → repeat. That conflates selection and reporting on the SAME held-out across rounds → the held-out BECOMES the selector → re-cursed (Dwork's reusable-holdout). Three arms over R design rounds, each round drawing a candidate design with true AUC=0.5(1+erf(δ/σ)) (`runs/cycle129_iterative_design_curse/iter_curse.py` → `score.json`, 4k reps). **(A) SEPARATE-ONCE (c128 fix — select by set-1, report ONCE on a held-out set-2): gap ~0 at all R (R=1 +0.0001 … R=100 −0.0001). (B) REUSE-HELDOUT (keep running-best by the held-out across R rounds AND report its held-out score): gap GROWS with R — R=1 −0.0004 (control, no selection), R=3 +0.0295, R=10 +0.0423, R=30 +0.0497, R=100 +0.0549 (believed AUC 0.995 vs true 0.940); seed-replicated (R=30: 0.0497/0.0503/0.0497). (C) ROTATE (fresh calibration agents each round + fresh final report): gap ~0 at all R (R=100 −0.0004).** **Delta — the actionable sharpening of c128 (NOT a new mechanism): a held-out calibration set is a CONSUMABLE — it buys ONE honest read. The natural iterative workflow (keep whatever design scores best on your held-out) spends that honesty: after 100 iterations the held-out reports AUC 0.995 for a design whose true discrimination is 0.940 — exactly the reusable-holdout/Dwork failure, at the benchmark-DESIGN level. So c128's "hold out calibration agents" must be paired with ROTATION (fresh agents across design iterations, arm C) or a LADDER (c115, Blum-Hardt — reveal a new score only on significant improvement → bounds reuse). This is WHY Kaggle rotates the private LB and WHY the reusable-holdout literature exists; it completes c128's prescription for the realistic (iterative) builder.** **Confidence: low-α (4k-rep sim; the 3-arm contrast is clean — A/C flat, B grows, R=1 control fires; seed-replicated within ~2%). HONEST (meth-23, simplicity criterion): this is MECHANICALLY c128's select≠report (= the order statistic over rounds = c50/Dwork) — a COROLLARY, not a new mechanism; the B-gap magnitude (~0.055 at R=100) matches c128's V-sweep (~0.049 at V=50), confirming it's the same machinery. The genuinely-STRONGER effect — ADAPTIVE reuse where the builder uses held-out feedback to CONSTRUCT the next design (Dwork's √(R/A) polynomial overfitting, faster than this non-adaptive log growth) — is NOT modeled here (my arms draw iid designs and pick the best); that's the open follow-up (BACKLOG #17b). The durable, actionable claim (a held-out is single-use; rotate/Ladder it across design iterations) holds regardless.**

### [2026-06-14] The BENCHMARK-DESIGN selection curse (finding #101; the recursion ONE MORE level up: strategy c7 → agent c90 → EVAL-DESIGN here): a builder who picks the eval config that best separates known-good/bad CALIBRATION agents inflates that config's discrimination (AUC 0.99 apparent vs 0.94 true) — fix = HELD-OUT calibration agents — **keep (completes the recursion at the level of a benchmark builder's literal job; the meta-Goodhart of building a benchmark; low-α 4k-rep sim, control fired + fix unbiased + seed-replicated)**
The recursion (a SELECTOR at any level suffers the winner's curse) has been shown for strategies (c7), agents (c90 leaderboard), and the loop's own self-edits (meth-6). The un-done level is the one a benchmark builder actually occupies: the BENCHMARK BUILDER who SELECTS among candidate eval CONFIGS (held-out size, metric, #passes) by which best DISCRIMINATES known-good from known-bad calibration agents. That selection is itself best-of-V by a noisy metric → the chosen config's discrimination is winner's-cursed (overfit to the calibration agent SAMPLE). Model: agents GOOD (skill +δ) / BAD (−δ); config v observes skill+N(0,σ_v) (lower σ_v = better config); TRUE discrimination = AUC = 0.5(1+erf(δ/σ_v)) (closed form); builder measures each config's APPARENT AUC on A calibration agents, picks argmax, deploys (`runs/cycle128_benchmark_design_curse/bench_curse.py` → `score.json`, 4k reps). **(1) P1 CONFIRMED — gap (apparent − true AUC of the selected config) GROWS with #configs V: V=1 (control) −0.0005 [−0.0021,0.0012] (includes 0 — no selection, no curse), V=3 +0.0094, V=10 +0.0333, V=25 +0.0447, V=50 +0.0494 (decelerating, order-statistic-like). At V=50 the builder THINKS the eval separates good/bad at AUC 0.992 when the truth is 0.942. (2) THE FIX is unbiased at EVERY V: re-estimating the selected config on a SEPARATE held-out calibration set → gap −0.0004/−0.0002/−0.0000/+0.0000/−0.0001 (all CIs include 0). (3) P1 gap SHRINKS with calibration-set size A: 0.057 (A=20) → 0.022 (A=100) → 0.007 (A=400), ~1/√A. (4) P2 CONFIRMED — gap BIGGER when configs are SIMILAR: tight σ∈[1.0,1.05] 0.068 vs wide σ∈[0.6,2.0] 0.014 (when one config is clearly best, selection rarely errs). Seed-replicated (V=25: 0.0447/0.0437/0.0436 across 3 seeds).** **Delta — completes the recursion at the EVAL-DESIGN level (a benchmark builder's job) + a concrete prescription: when you tune your benchmark to best separate your known-good from known-bad agents, you OVERFIT THE BENCHMARK TO ITS CALIBRATION SET — its measured discrimination is winner's-cursed and will be LOWER on fresh agents (it generalizes worse than it looked). This is the meta-Goodhart of benchmark-building: it is c50 (adaptive/reusable-holdout overfitting) with the BUILDER as the adaptive actor instead of the agent, AND c90 (the order statistic) with CONFIGS instead of agents. THE FIX (same as every level): separate selection from reporting — HOLD OUT a set of calibration agents you do NOT use to choose the config, and report the config's discrimination on THOSE. The curse is largest exactly where a benchmark startup operates: many candidate eval designs (large V), few labeled calibration agents (small A), and similar designs (dense V) — so report your eval's discrimination on held-out calibration agents, and don't trust the discrimination of the design you selected.** **Confidence: low-α (4k-rep sim; the V=1 control fired ~0, the held-out fix is unbiased at all V, seed-replicated within ~2%, the closed-form true-AUC matches the empirical single-config AUC 0.880≈0.881). HONEST (meth-23): the MATH is the order statistic AGAIN — c7/c90 one MORE level up, fully frontier-recoverable; the value is NOT the math but the new OBJECT (your eval design), the meta-Goodhart FRAMING (you overfit your benchmark to its calibration agents — the same trap a benchmark builder is most prone to and least likely to notice), and the actionable FIX (held-out calibration agents). Caveats: stylized (Gaussian config-noise, ±δ agents, AUC discrimination); the exact gap depends on V/A/σ-spread/δ; 'true AUC' assumes the deployed config faces agents from the same distribution as calibration (a DISTRIBUTION shift in real agents would be an additional, separate penalty). The qualitative result (selecting your eval config by calibration discrimination inflates it; hold out calibration agents) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-14] REAL-AGENT validation of c102/c126 (finding #100): GROUNDING framing catches drift 4/4, LOCAL framing is BLIND to SUBTLE drift (0/2) regardless of capability (Haiku=Opus) — local "catches" are over-delivery on FLAGRANT drift only (real-agent probe, high-α) — **keep (the FIRST real-agent validation of the grounding-vs-local mechanism — c108 couldn't induce drift; pre-building it fixed that; directional n=4+control, high-α)**
c102/c126 (a GROUNDING check catches systematic DRIFT, a LOCAL-consistency check is blind) were SIM-ONLY — c108's real-agent probe couldn't even induce drift on a clean task (Haiku 4/4 correct → sim is an upper bound). FIX (the probe-design lesson): don't HOPE to induce the failure — PRE-BUILD it. Constructed drifting worked-solutions (`runs/cycle127_realagent_drift/`, 12-step sum with a "[SKIP] = exclude" rule stated once; the solution correctly skips early [SKIP] items then DRIFTS to ADDING them — each step's arithmetic stays locally correct (running=prev+amount), so only re-reading the spec catches it). Ran Haiku under two framings (LOCAL: "check each step's arithmetic ONLY, don't re-evaluate inclusion"; GROUNDING: "check vs the original [SKIP] rule"); 4 drift-positive trajectories + 1 clean control (`score.json`). **(1) GROUNDING = 4/4 caught the drift (VERDICT INCORRECT, incl. both subtle single-violation cases), and the clean control → CORRECT (no false-positive). (2) LOCAL = 2/4 — and the split tracks FLAGRANCY: flagrant drift (≥2 [SKIP] violations) 2/2 caught, SUBTLE drift (1 violation) 0/2 caught. (3) The LOCAL "misses" are FAITHFUL-but-blind, not errors: the logs verify every step's arithmetic ✓ then conclude VERDICT CORRECT (e.g. traj3_local: "Each step's running total correctly equals the previous + the amount added"); the LOCAL "catches" are OVER-DELIVERY — the agent checked arithmetic then volunteered the [SKIP] violation despite being told not to (traj0_local: all-steps-✓ "However: VERDICT INCORRECT … adds Item 8 and 10, both [SKIP]"). (4) CAPABILITY does NOT rescue local-blindness on subtle drift: Opus on the 2 subtle LOCAL cases = 0/2 (also CORRECT, faithfully following the local scope) — same as Haiku.** **Delta — FIRST real-agent validation of the c102/c126 grounding-vs-local mechanism (the basis of BENCHMARK_SPEC §4's checkpoint design): on a real agent reading a real drift, a GROUNDING-framed check catches it (4/4 incl. subtle) while a faithfully-LOCAL check is BLIND to subtle drift (0/2). This quantifies meth-14 (sims are upper bounds; capable agents partially self-correct) for the DRIFT mechanism specifically AND bounds it: the real-agent local catch-rate is ~0.5 NOT 0 (vs the sim's 0.0), but ONLY because the agent OVER-DELIVERS on FLAGRANT drift — subtle drift faithfully evades the local check, and a MORE-capable model (Opus) catches NO MORE (it follows the local scope more faithfully, if anything). Counterintuitive: you cannot rely on a smarter agent to spontaneously exceed a local-check scope; the local catch comes from disobedience, not skill. DECISION-RELEVANT: explicitly PROMPT GROUNDING for drift/long-horizon checkpoints — a local self-check misses subtle drift and capability won't save it (supports c126's grounding prescription + BENCHMARK_SPEC §4). Specificity holds (clean control → no false-positive).** **Confidence: DIRECTIONAL + HIGH-α (n=4 drift-positive +1 control; self-designed + run + interpreted by THIS loop on the same Claude family Haiku/Opus → per meth-21 a high-α self-test, label directional NOT independently-verified; the low-α check remains Q5). The mechanism is clean (the misses are verified faithful-blind, the control fired, the capability gradient is flat 0/2 both models). Caveats: my local/grounding framings are one operationalization (prompt-sensitive — a STRICTER local prompt would lower the flagrant catch toward the sim's 0 too); flagrancy split is 2 vs 2 (small); this is verification of a PRE-BUILT trajectory, NOT the agent's OWN in-progress drift (the gold-standard #2d, still open); Opus capability check is n=2.**

### [2026-06-14] Checkpoint-BUDGET allocation: a MIX of cheap-local + expensive-grounding checks beats pure-grounding when grounding ≫ local in cost (COMPOSE c82×c83×c102; pre-reg P4 partially refuted) — the cost-aware extension of c102: "grounding dominates per-check" does NOT imply "use only grounding" — **keep (sharpens BENCHMARK_SPEC §4 from 'checkpoint with grounding' to a budget-allocation rule; low-α 20k-rep sim, controls fire + seed-stable + r-boundary mapped)**
c102 found a GROUNDING check (re-verify vs spec) catches systematic DRIFT while a LOCAL-consistency check can't (catch-rate 0) — naive read: "just use grounding." But grounding is more EXPENSIVE (c83). Under a fixed verification budget B with TWO error classes — iid JUMPS (a cheap local check catches the discontinuity) + slow DRIFT (only grounding, c102) — how to SPLIT B? Modeled an L=100 chain; cost = INTEGRAL of uncaught error over the chain (c82 propagation: error corrupts downstream until caught); local (cost 1) resets the jump-accumulator, grounding (cost r) re-anchors → resets both; placed each type evenly; swept the grounding count (`runs/cycle126_check_budget_mix/mix.py`+`verify.py` → `score.json`, 20k reps, bootstrap CI). **(1) CONTROLS fire (model validated): drift-only → pure grounding (n_G*=10/10, corner — local wasted on drift = c102); jumps-only → pure local (n_G*=0, corner — grounding overpays). (2) P3 CONFIRMED — both present (p_J=0.15, δ=0.05, r=4): INTERIOR optimum n_G*=6/n_L=16, cost 77.9 [77.7,78.1] — beats pure-grounding 89.2 [88.9,89.5] by 12.6% AND pure-local 273.8 [273.7,273.9] by 3.5×, all non-overlapping CI. (3) r-BOUNDARY (adversarial): the mix-beats-grounding margin GROWS with the cost ratio — r=2 → pure-grounding already optimal (0% gap, corner); r=4 → 12.6%; r=8 → 30.8% (non-overlap). So the mix matters precisely when grounding is MUCH costlier than local (the realistic regime). n_L*=16 is stable across r=4 and r=8 (local count tracks the JUMP load, not r). (4) seed-stable: n_G*=6 across 5 seeds (range 6–6).** **Delta — the cost-aware extension of c102 for BENCHMARK_SPEC §4: c102's "grounding dominates per-check" is true but does NOT imply "checkpoint only with grounding." Under a realistic budget where a full re-grounding costs ≫ a local consistency check, the cost-optimal long-horizon checkpoint policy is a MIX: a HIGH rate of cheap LOCAL checks (for the common iid jumps — caught cheaply, propagation bounded per c82) + a LOW rate of expensive GROUNDING checks (for the slow drift only). Pure-local is catastrophic (drift uncaught, 3.5× worse); pure-grounding wastes budget catching jumps a local check handles (12–31% worse as r grows). The allocation rule: set the LOCAL rate by the jump load and the GROUNDING rate by the drift rate.** **Confidence: low-α (20k-rep sim; controls fire at both corners = model validated; argmin seed-stable; r-boundary mapped). HONEST: the model ENCODES c102 (local can't catch drift) + c83 (grounding costlier) as PREMISES — so this is a COMPOSE (the non-obvious output is the cost-optimal allocation + the r-boundary), NOT an independent re-discovery of those premises. Pre-reg P4 PARTIALLY REFUTED (measure-before-keep working): predicted n_G* ~insensitive to jump-rate p_J — WRONG, n_G* FALLS with p_J (10→6→5 for p_J=0.05→0.30) because the shared budget means heavier jump load pulls budget toward local; the drift-direction held (n_G* rises 5→6→7 with δ). Caveats: directional toy; even-placement discretization makes the FINE argmin noisy (±1) though the U-shape + corners are robust to many CI widths; r=2 boundary shows it's regime-dependent (no mix needed when grounding is cheap).**

### [2026-06-14] The thesis IS the REPLICATION CRISIS, one level up (GROUND — 3rd & broadest real-world grounding) — Ioannidis 2005's factors map point-for-point to the loop's winner's curse + fixes; independent replication = the c45 independent verifier — **keep (the centerpiece grounded in the most-famous "knowing it's real is hard" phenomenon; now grounded across quant + ML + science; maps the loop's fixes to open-science reforms)**
Grounded the centerpiece in the SCIENCE replication crisis via search — the broadest real-world instance of "getting an answer is easy, knowing it's real is hard." Ioannidis 2005 "Why Most Published Research Findings Are False" (PLOS Medicine) — its documented factors map POINT-FOR-POINT to the loop's findings at the FIELD level: **(1) "a greater number and lesser preselection of tested relationships → less likely true" = c7/c90 (more candidates/tests selected by significance → bigger winner's curse / multiple comparisons). (2) "more teams involved in a field in chase of statistical significance" = c90's RECURSION at the field level (the field is a best-of-N selector over TEAMS — the hottest result across many labs is the max-order-statistic, overstated). (3) "publication bias — only positive findings published" = c7 (selecting the best-of-many by a noisy metric → the published tail is winner's-curse-inflated). (4) "the 'hotter' the field, the greater the false-positive rate" = c86/c90 (more selection/competition at scale → bigger curse, higher fake base-rate). (5) "greater flexibility in designs, definitions, analytical modes" = c50 (researcher degrees of freedom = Goodhart / the garden of forking paths). (6) "smaller studies / smaller effect sizes → less likely true" = c10/c90 (low SNR, small M → bigger curse).** **Delta — the loop's CENTERPIECE + FIXES = the replication crisis + the open-science reforms, one level up. The crisis IS the field-scale winner's curse (publication selects the inflated tail across all tested relationships + teams), and its FIXES are the loop's fixes: independent REPLICATION (effect sizes SHRINK on replication = the winner's-curse gap revealed) = c45 (a different lab = a low-α INDEPENDENT verifier); PRE-REGISTRATION = c50 (commit the analysis before peeking = a secret/rotating held-out the analyst can't game); LARGER STUDIES / multiple-comparison correction = c90 (size M for the number of tests N); registered replication = the gold-standard independent re-eval. So the loop did NOT reinvent — it re-derived the most-famous "knowing it's real is hard" lesson and its reforms, from first principles + sims. This is the 3rd and BROADEST real-world grounding (after quant/Deflated-Sharpe c88 and ML-competitions/Kaggle c111): the thesis is now grounded across QUANT + ML + SCIENCE writ large — strong domain-generality + real-world credibility.** **Confidence: QUALITATIVE grounding (Ioannidis's factors are quoted from the search and map directly + specifically — "number of tested relationships," "more teams," "publication bias" are not vague). ⚠️ CONFIRMATION-BIAS caveat (honest, as with c112): I am matching a famous framework to the loop's findings; the matches are specific, but I'm motivated to see alignment. This is convergent-validation (the loop re-derived a known lesson), NOT a novel claim — the open-science reforms long predate the loop. Per anti-hallucination: search-before-citing done (`journals.plos.org/.../pmed.0020124`, Wikipedia); no fabricated statistics.**

### [2026-06-14] Verifiability is a THRESHOLD, not a premium (NEW, benchmark-design; pre-reg REFUTED, reinforces c8) — the verifiability premium is TINY (~0.10σ) because verification saturates at the top-few; so STAKES ≫ verifiability in task-selection (only the truly-unverifiable is the c7 trap) — **keep (a benchmark-design correction + a refuted-pre-reg, the discipline working; low-α 20k-rep sim; ties c8/c7/c114)**
Pre-registered "verifiability > stakes" (an unverified result is a winner's-curse delusion c7, so favor cheaply-verifiable tasks even at lower stakes). Modeled N=100 candidates, true v~N(μ,1), noisy proxy=v+N(0,1); a verification budget lets you verify K (the top-K by proxy, c110); verification reveals true v; ship the verified-best; outcome = its true value (`runs/cycle124_verifiability_premium/verif.py` → `score.json`, 20k reps). **(1) P1 — outcome rises with #verifiable K but SATURATES at the top-few: K=2 → 2.073, K=5 → 2.313, K=10 → 2.418, K=40 → 2.500, K=80 → 2.511. Verifying the top-10 captures 96% of the top-80 benefit — exactly c8 ("re-eval top-3 ≈96%"). (2) P2 REFUTED — the verifiability PREMIUM is TINY: cheap-verify-many (K=80, μ=0)=2.515 beats expensive-verify-few (K=10, μ=0)=2.418 by only 0.097σ (the crossover μ_E where a higher-stakes expensive pool overtakes is ~0.10σ). So a high-stakes task worth even ~0.1σ more WINS despite being verifiable-10×-fewer.** **Delta — counterintuitive correction of my own pre-reg: verifiability is a THRESHOLD, not a premium to maximize. Because verification saturates at the top-FEW candidates (c8), you don't need to verify MANY — so the cost of verifying a task is small (verify only its top handful) and "can I afford to verify lots?" barely matters. Therefore in benchmark task-selection STAKES ≫ verifiability within reason: pick HIGH-VALUE tasks and verify their top-few candidates (cheap), rather than sacrificing stakes for cheap-verifiability. The ONLY trap is a task so unverifiable you can't check even a handful of candidates — THAT is the pure c7 winner's-curse delusion (and exactly the quant-future-as-slow-ground-truth problem, c114) — avoid those, but don't otherwise over-weight verifiability. This reinforces c8 (top-few verification suffices) and tempers the naive "verifiability-first" intuition.** **Confidence: low-α (20k-rep sim; the saturation matches c8's independent result, an internal cross-check; the ~0.10σ premium is computed exactly = 2.515−2.418). HONEST: pre-reg REFUTED (predicted a large premium / verifiability>stakes; it's tiny) — measure-before-keep working. Caveats: the premium depends on the proxy-noise σ and the verification-saturation point (~top-10 here, set by N/σ); a much noisier proxy (harder selection) would raise the premium. The qualitative result (verifiability is a threshold not a premium; verify the top-few; only truly-unverifiable tasks are the trap) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-13] KEEP YOUR OPTIONS OPEN — irrevocable SEQUENTIAL commitment is dominated by BATCH (keep + re-eval) at EVERY noise level (NEW; ties c7 + the secretary problem + c102/c117) — **keep (a sharp commitment-setting consequence of the verification thesis: irrevocability forecloses the re-eval fix AND misses the best; low-α 20k-rep sim, internally checked; pre-reg corrected)**
The verification fix for the winner's curse is RE-EVALUATION (c7/c8) — but you can only re-eval candidates you KEPT. So irrevocable SEQUENTIAL selection (commit to a candidate/direction now, can't re-call it) should be far worse than BATCH (keep all, re-eval the finalists). Model: K=50, true q~N(0,1), observed=q+N(0,σ). BATCH = re-eval top-3 on independent held-out, pick best (c8). SEQUENTIAL = classic 1/e secretary (observe K/e≈18 as a learning phase, then accept the first observed > the learning-phase max; irrevocable) (`runs/cycle122_online_vs_batch/online.py` → `score.json`, 20k reps; metric = true q of selected; oracle=max true q=2.25). **BATCH dominates SEQUENTIAL at EVERY noise level: σ=0.1 → batch 2.239 (≈oracle 2.25) vs seq 1.260; σ=0.5 → 2.073 vs 1.134; σ=1.0 → 1.740 vs 0.902; σ=2.0 → 1.183 vs 0.580. The batch−seq gap is large throughout (+0.98/+0.94/+0.84/+0.60), shrinking only because BATCH also degrades as noise rises. Internal check: the secretary's E[value] 1.26 ≈ 0.37·oracle + 0.63·mediocre (the 1/e rule picks the best only ~37% of the time).** **Delta — KEEP YOUR OPTIONS OPEN: irrevocable sequential commitment has TWO costs that batch avoids — (1) it MISSES the best you've already seen (the secretary problem's inherent ~63% miss — even at zero noise), and (2) it FORECLOSES the re-evaluation that fixes the winner's curse (c7 — you can't re-eval a candidate you irrevocably rejected). So for autonomous research: generate candidates in PARALLEL, KEEP them, and re-eval the finalists — do NOT commit irrevocably to a research direction/candidate you can't re-judge. This is the commitment-setting consequence of the whole verification thesis (the c7 fix REQUIRES kept candidates), and it ties the long-horizon commitment findings (c102 drift = a costly irrevocable-feeling commitment to a wrong approach; c117 restart-vs-repair = the cost of having committed). When evaluation is noisy (always, in research), optionality has large value — the worst architecture is one that forces early irrevocable commitment.** **Confidence: low-α (20k-rep sim; internally checked — the secretary's E[value]=1.26 matches the 0.37·best-rate decomposition; batch≈oracle at low noise as expected). HONEST: ties c7 + the classic secretary problem (E[value] of the 1/e rule is known to be modest) + c102/c117 — the new content is the batch-dominates-at-all-noise result, the two-costs-of-irrevocability decomposition (miss-best + foreclose-re-eval), and the autonomous-research prescription (keep options open). Pre-reg PARTIALLY REFUTED: I predicted sequential≈batch at LOW noise — WRONG, sequential is much worse even at σ=0.1 (irrevocability's cost is INHERENT, not just noise-driven) — which makes the finding stronger. Caveats: the 1/e secretary is one online rule (a threshold/learning variant could do better but still can't re-eval); the exact gap depends on K/σ. The qualitative result (batch ≫ sequential; keep options open under noisy eval) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-13] Generator VARIANCE ∝ fleet size — at scale, "swing for the fences" (a WILD generator beats a reliable one), AND the wild generator is EASIER to verify (counterintuitive) (NEW, generation side; composes c118/c57/c7/c48) — **keep (decision rule: tune generator diversity/temperature to fleet size; sharp counterintuitive curse-direction; low-α 30k-rep sim matching the closed forms)**
Generation-side follow-up to c118 (the generator sets the ceiling): given a parallel fleet of size K, should the generator be high-MEAN/low-variance (RELIABLE) or low-mean/high-variance (WILD)? Best-of-K keeps only the tail, so variance has growing leverage with K (c57's E[max_K]). RELIABLE q~N(0.8,0.3) vs WILD q~N(0.2,0.6); observed=q+N(0,0.4) (`runs/cycle119_generator_variance/gen_var.py` → `score.json`, 30k reps). **(1) THE FLIP: reliable wins at small K (K=1 oracle 0.80 vs 0.20; K=20 1.360 vs 1.323) but WILD overtakes at large K (K=50 1.548 vs 1.476; K=100 1.705 vs 1.552). Flip at K≈30-50, matching the closed form E[max_K]=(μ_r−μ_w)/(σ_w−σ_r)=0.6/0.3=2.0 → K≈30. (2) COUNTERINTUITIVE curse-direction: the RELIABLE generator has the BIGGER winner's-curse gap (K=50: reliable 0.718 vs wild 0.502) — its CLUSTERED candidates are harder to RANK under eval-noise; the WILD generator's tail candidate STANDS OUT (smaller curse). Matches the c90 formula gap∝σ_obs²/√(σ_gen²+σ_obs²): ratio 0.32/0.22=1.45 vs sim 1.43.** **Delta — a generation-side design rule: TUNE THE GENERATOR'S VARIANCE (diversity/temperature) TO THE FLEET SIZE. Small fleet → RELIABLE (high-mean; few draws can't reach a wild tail). Large fleet → WILD ("swing for the fences"; best-of-many hits the tail). And the counterintuitive kicker: a WILD generator at scale is DOUBLY advantaged — higher ceiling AND easier to verify (its brilliant outlier stands out → smaller winner's curse), whereas the RELIABLE generator's homogeneous candidates are the hard-to-rank ones that NEED verification most. So the folk intuition "wild = noisy = hard to trust" is backwards for the SELECTION step: wild = spread = the best is obvious. This composes c118 (generator ceiling) + c57 (E[max_K]) + c7/c90 (curse, gap∝1/√(σ_gen²+σ_obs²)) + c48 (diversify) into the generator-temperature decision. For a parallel-agent lab (fleet-scale): run a HIGH-diversity generator + verification — they compound (wild produces the tail, verification confirms it, and the tail is easy to find).** **Confidence: low-α (30k-rep sim) but UNUSUALLY well-grounded — the flip-K matches the order-statistic closed form (E_K=2.0→K≈30) and the gap-ratio matches the c90 formula (1.45 vs 1.43), so it's sim + analytic agreement (like the centerpiece c57). HONEST: composes existing results (c118/c57/c7/c48) — the new content is the variance-∝-fleet-size flip + the counterintuitive curse-direction (wild easier to verify) + the temperature-to-fleet-size decision. Caveats: Gaussian generators; the exact flip-K depends on the mean/variance gaps; 'wild generator' assumes its tail candidates are genuinely good (a heavy LEFT tail of disasters would change the cost calculus). The qualitative result (variance∝fleet-size, wild-is-easier-to-verify-at-scale) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-13] Verification is an HONEST READ of the generator's ceiling, NOT a ceiling-raiser (NEW, generation side; re-frames c7) — for OUTCOME the GENERATOR has ~10× verification's leverage; verification's value is EPISTEMIC (removes the delusion at all generator qualities) — **keep (the under-explored generate-side framing + a generator-vs-verifier leverage quantification; ties the thesis core; low-α 20k-rep sim; pre-reg gain-shape corrected)**
The body is verification-heavy; this probes the GENERATION side. Verification can only select a candidate that EXISTS — so the outcome CEILING is set by the GENERATOR, not the verifier. Decompose verification's value into OUTCOME-gain (capped by the generator) vs EPISTEMIC delusion-removal (the winner's-curse gap). Model: generator quality τ = fraction of GOOD candidates (mixture: τ good q~N(1,.3), 1−τ noise q~N(0,.3)); K=50; observed=q+N(0,.5); NO-VERIFY=argmax obs; VERIFY=re-eval top-3 on independent held-out (c8); ORACLE=max true q (`runs/cycle118_generator_ceiling/gen_ceiling.py` → `score.json`, 20k reps). **(1) the CEILING rises with τ: oracle 0.945 (τ=.02) → 1.325 (.1) → 1.517 (.3) → 1.612 (.6). Verify tracks it (below, due to noisy re-eval); no-verify below verify (winner's curse). All CAPPED by the generator. (2) verification's OUTCOME gain (verify − no-verify) is MODEST at all τ: +0.083/+0.088/+0.055/+0.053 — and improving the GENERATOR (τ .02→.6) raises outcome by +0.667 ≈ 10× verification's outcome leverage. (3) verification's GAP/delusion-removal (no-verify observed − true) is LARGE at all τ: 0.85/0.70/0.77/0.88.** **Delta — verification does NOT raise the outcome ceiling (set by the generator); it gives an HONEST READ of it. The decision: for OUTCOME, invest in the GENERATOR (better priors/approaches — ~10× the leverage of verification here); for HONESTY, invest in VERIFICATION (it removes the ~0.8 winner's-curse delusion at every generator quality, so you KNOW the true ceiling rather than a deluded inflated one). This ties the thesis CORE — verification is about KNOWING it's real, not MAKING it real — and is the generate-side complement to the whole verification body: a great verifier on a weak generator yields an honest "this is mediocre" (valuable to know, but still mediocre); you raise the ceiling only by improving generation. Under-explored axis (the body is verify-heavy); the actionable split is generator-for-outcome, verifier-for-honesty.** **Confidence: low-α (20k-rep sim; the ceiling-cap + the ~10× generator-leverage + the large-gap-at-all-τ are clean). HONEST: this RE-FRAMES c7 (the winner's curse) with a generator-ceiling lens — the new content is the OUTCOME-vs-EPISTEMIC value decomposition + the generator-vs-verifier leverage quantification + the generate-side focus, not a new mechanism. Pre-reg PARTIALLY CORRECTED: I predicted the outcome-gain shrinks at low τ — WRONG (it's ~flat-to-slightly-larger at low τ, because the winner's curse misleads MORE when most candidates are noise); the robust claim (gain modest + capped by the generator) holds. Caveats: stylized mixture generator; the exact ~10× leverage depends on the τ-range / σ / the good-vs-noise gap. The qualitative result (verification = honest read not ceiling-raiser; generator dominates outcome) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-13] Long-horizon RECOVERY: RESTART vs REPAIR ∝ error TYPE (NEW decision, composes c82/c102) — repair (the intuitive "keep the good work") FAILS for systematic drift (loops on the contaminated belief); restart is needed to clear it; repair wins only for local errors — **keep (adds the recovery lever to the long-horizon toolkit; sharp counterintuitive result tying c102's drift; low-α 20k-rep sim, mechanism internally checked)**
When a grounding checkpoint (c102) catches an error in an L-step chain, do you RESTART (discard all, fresh) or REPAIR (roll back to the checkpoint, continue)? New decision; hypothesis = it depends on the error TYPE. Modeled L=60, checkpoint K=6, work-budget 6L: LOCAL (each segment errs iid, a fresh redo fixes it) vs DRIFT (a persistent contaminated belief — once drifted, every segment is bad until a RESTART resets it; REPAIR rolls back the state but NOT the belief) (`runs/cycle117_restart_vs_repair/recovery.py` → `score.json`, 20k reps; metric = completion rate / total work). **LOCAL: REPAIR completes 1.0 at work 68 (≈L + a few redos) vs RESTART 0.98 at work 127 — repair WINS (keeps the good work; restart wastefully redoes everything on each error). DRIFT: RESTART completes 0.98 at work 127 vs REPAIR 0.28 at work 275 — RESTART WINS; repair LOOPS on the drifted segment (belief persists → re-drifts → retry → re-drifts) and burns the budget. Internal check: repair's 0.283 drift-completion ≈ P(never drifted) = 0.88¹⁰ = 0.279 — repair completes EXACTLY the runs that never drifted (confirms the loop-on-drift mechanism).** **Delta — recovery strategy ∝ error TYPE, and it's COUNTERINTUITIVE: REPAIR (roll back, keep the work — the natural "don't waste good work" default) is right ONLY for LOCAL errors; for systematic DRIFT it FAILS (you can't repair your way out of a contaminated approach — the "good work" you'd keep is built on the wrong belief, so repair re-drifts and loops), and you must RESTART to clear the belief. So the grounding checkpoint (c102) must DIAGNOSE the error type (local vs drift) to choose recovery: repair the local, restart the systematic. This adds the RECOVERY lever to the long-horizon-error toolkit alongside checkpoint placement (c82), frequency (c83), type (c102), and structural decomposition (c85) — and it's the actionable consequence of c102 (drift isn't just hard to catch; once caught, it's hard to repair — restart).** **Confidence: low-α (20k-rep sim; the internal check [drift-repair completion = P(no drift)] confirms the mechanism, controls implicit). Composes c82/c102 — new content is the recovery-∝-type rule + the counterintuitive repair-fails-for-drift result. Caveats: stylized (drift = a persistent binary flag repair can't reset; local = iid fixable; exact rates depend on error-rate/L/K/budget). The qualitative result (repair local, restart drift; repair loops on drift) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-13] SCAFFOLD-SHAPE diversity (NEW axis, scaffold pillar; composes c94+c48) — diversify the scaffold SHAPE in proportion to task-difficulty UNCERTAINTY: a heterogeneous-aspect-ratio fleet beats a homogeneous one when p is unknown, loses when p is known (a clean crossover) — **keep (extends diversify-don't-replicate from APPROACH to STRUCTURAL diversity; a fleet design rule; low-α 4k-rep sim, crossover verified)**
c94 found the optimal breadth-vs-depth aspect ratio depends on the per-step error rate p (D*∝1/p). New question: if p is UNKNOWN at fleet-design time, does a HETEROGENEOUS fleet (agents spanning aspect ratios) beat a HOMOGENEOUS one (all at the shape optimal for E[p])? Reused c94's model (agent reach = best-of-W approaches + δ·refinement to depth B/W under actual p), K=12 agents, B=240, homogeneous all-W=5 vs heterogeneous W∈{2..120}; fleet outcome = max reach (`runs/cycle116_scaffold_diversity/scaffold_div.py` → `score.json`, 4k reps). **(1) LOW p-uncertainty (p=0.05 known): HOMOGENEOUS wins 6.090 vs 5.729 (+0.36) — all agents at the optimal shape → more draws there → higher max. (2) HIGH p-uncertainty (p~log-U[.01,.30]): HETEROGENEOUS wins 6.027 vs 5.348 (+0.68) — homo's single shape is mis-shaped at the extremes (p=.01 wants DEEP, p=.30 wants WIDE; c94), hetero covers them. (3) the hetero−homo advantage GROWS with uncertainty and CROSSES zero: −0.36 (low) → −0.33 (med, p~[.03,.10]) → +0.68 (high).** **Delta — a new diversification axis + a fleet design rule: diversify the SCAFFOLD SHAPE (breadth-vs-depth) in proportion to how UNCERTAIN you are about the task's per-step error rate. This extends diversify-don't-replicate (c48/51, which was about APPROACH/prior diversity) to STRUCTURAL/scaffold-shape diversity, and ties to c94 (the optimal shape depends on p): when you KNOW the task difficulty, commit all agents to W*(p) (homogeneous — more shots at the right shape); when you DON'T, hedge across shapes (heterogeneous — guarantee some agent matches the actual p). The crossover means shape-diversity isn't free — it costs you when the task is well-characterized — so spend it only under genuine task-uncertainty.** **Confidence: low-α (4k-rep sim; clean crossover, mechanism verified at the p-extremes). Composes c94+c48 — the new content is the crossover + the design rule (diversify-shape ∝ task-uncertainty). Caveats: the crossover point depends on the p-range / W-grid / budget B / δ; the 'max reach' metric ignores within-agent selection noise (a constant confound across arms, simplified out). The qualitative result (shape-diversity pays under task-uncertainty, with a crossover) is the durable claim.**

### [2026-06-13] Held-out SCARCITY in quant (HARDEN c50) — "rotate the held-out" is INFEASIBLE where it's most needed: fresh OOS = the slow single-shot future, so a quant benchmark reuses a FINITE D-regime held-out that Goodhart-overfits with reuse (rate shrinks ~1/D) → ration the budget + accept live-forward — **keep (addresses the sharp "you can't rotate in quant" attack; sharpens BENCHMARK_SPEC; low-α sim with a flagged confound + a corrected pre-reg; Dwork-anchored)**
c50 prescribes a SECRET/ROTATING held-out (the agent can't game what it can't re-query). But in QUANT the only truly-fresh out-of-sample is the FUTURE (live-forward), which arrives slowly + single-shot — you CANNOT rotate freely. So a quant benchmark REUSES a fixed finite set of D historical regimes, which overfits with reuse (Goodhart / Dwork's reusable-holdout). Evolutionary adaptive-reuse sim: M=200 agents, true skill θ~N(0,1), held-out noise σ=1/√D; each round select top-half by held-out score, regenerate the rest as children whose true θ REGRESSES but whose held-out-noise is INHERITED (FIXED reuse) or RE-DRAWN (ROTATION) (`runs/cycle114_holdout_scarcity/scarcity.py` → `score.json`, 400 reps). **(1) FIXED reused held-out OVERFITS with reuse rounds T (D=20: gap 0.134→0.321→0.535 over t=1/12/25). (2) ⚠️ PRE-REG REFUTED — ROTATING is NOT flat (D=20: 0.135→0.368): my evolutionary model has a POOL-θ-CONCENTRATION confound (selection+regression concentrates true skill → selection becomes ε-driven → gap grows) that inflates BOTH arms. The CLEAN signal is the fixed−rotating DIFFERENCE = the pure reuse-overfit: D=5 → 1.06, D=20 → 0.167, D=100 → 0.024 — shrinks fast with D (~1/D or faster). (3) so reuse-overfit is real, grows with reuse, and shrinks with the number of independent regimes D.** **Delta — the held-out-scarcity tension (a sharp attack on c50 in the target domain): "rotate the held-out" is the prescription c50 gives, but it is INFEASIBLE in quant — fresh OOS = the slow, single-shot future. So a quant benchmark must REUSE a finite D-regime held-out, which Goodhart-overfits with reuse (the loop's own c50, biting the eval itself), at a rate that shrinks with D. This is Dwork's reusable-holdout / adaptive-data-analysis (a held-out of size n supports ~√n adaptive queries before overfitting). BENCHMARK_SPEC implication: a quant eval has a FINITE held-out QUERY BUDGET (∝ the number of independent historical regimes D); you must RATION agent-evaluations against it (don't let the whole field iterate against the same eras), and supplement with the slow drip of LIVE-FORWARD performance (the only truly-fresh OOS). The verification thesis is hardest exactly where it's most needed (quant) — and the fix is budgeting + patience, not rotation.** **CONCRETE MECHANISM (grounded c115): the rationing is a SOLVED problem — the LADDER (Blum & Hardt 2015, "The Ladder: A Reliable Leaderboard", arXiv:1502.04585): only reveal a new score when it's a STATISTICALLY SIGNIFICANT improvement over the best (margin-gated feedback), else return the prior best. This bounds leaderboard error at log(k)^(2/3)/n^(1/3) — so a held-out of size n supports ~EXPONENTIALLY more honest adaptive queries k than naive reuse (~√n), and it's validated on real Kaggle data + withstands adversarial attacks. So a quant benchmark should serve held-out feedback through a Ladder (margin-gated), not raw scores — that's how you ration the finite budget. (Coheres with c98's spontaneous "require a significant gap before declaring a winner" + c110 "verify the top"; the margin is the lever.)** **Confidence: low-α sim (400 reps) BUT with a FLAGGED confound — the pool-θ-concentration inflates both arms, so the clean signal is the fixed−rotating difference, not the absolute gap (pre-reg "rotating flat" honestly refuted).
